Linear response for Sinai billiards with small holes
Giovanni Canestrari

Abstract
. We show that the conditional survival probability measure for a Sinai billiard with a small hole on the boundary of the table is differentiable with respect to the size t t of the hole at t = 0 t=0 and we compute the derivative.



1.
Introduction

The importance of linear response is hard to overestimate. It is a central tool in statistical mechanics and condensed matter physics, climate science, and engineering (see, e.g., the introduction of
[
36
]
for an overview of the applications). However, from the mathematical point of view, the results are rather limited. In particular, in dynamical systems, there exist strong results only for cases in which the dynamics is smooth, and the perturbed system is conjugate to the original one. These are very strong limitations and a lot of energy has been devoted to trying to overcome them (see, e.g.​
[
46
,
5
,
7
]
). The present paper deals with billiards with holes: an example in which the system both has discontinuities and the perturbations are not conjugate to the original system, establishing, for the first time, a linear response formula. As the approach is rather general, it is likely to open the door to further applications.

The study of physically relevant invariant measures and their behavior under perturbations is a well-developed field in smooth ergodic theory and dynamical systems. Among different types of perturbations, a class with its own lore is obtained by opening holes in a dynamical system (see
[
3
]
for recent developments on the topic). A hole
{\mathcal{H}}
is a portion of the phase space
{\mathcal{M}}
with the property that trajectories entering
{\mathcal{H}}
are removed from the ensemble. If the system is chaotic enough, it is expected that Lebegue a.e.​ trajectory explores the whole phase space and eventually enters the hole. In this scenario, a natural object to look at are the so-called
conditionally invariant measures
. These are measures whose evolution, conditioned to the event of survival, is constant in time. The set of conditionally invariant measures is typically huge, as it contains, for example, periodic orbits that do not intersect the hole, but there are criteria to select measures considered more physically relevant. In particular, if the associated closed system preserves an SRB measure
\mu_{0}
, it is natural to consider the evolution of
\mu_{0}
conditioned to survival. Starting with
[
43
]
, it has been shown that in many instances such evolution converges to a conditionally invariant measure
\mu_{{\mathcal{H}}}
. (See, e.g.,
[
22
]
for expanding maps on the interval with Markovian and non-Markovian
[
48
,
28
]
holes,
[
19
,
20
,
18
,
17
]
for Anosov maps,
[
10
]
for non-uniformly hyperbolic settings). Typically, when this convergence takes place, one could start also from another initial ensemble different from
\mu_{0}
and obtain the same limit, and the convergence is exponentially fast for uniformly hyperbolic systems
[
23
,
40
]
. However, the maximal class of admissible initial measures is a subtle matter
[
27
]
.

Once existence of physically relevant conditionally invariant measures is established, the next immediate question is to understand their dependence on the hole. A way to formalize this is to consider a family
{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
of holes indexed by a real parameter
t
, select a smooth observable
\phi:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and study the regularity of the function
t\to\mu_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}}(\phi)
. In this context, typical results comprise continuity of
\mu_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}}
. See, for example,
[
26
]
for holes in piecewise
{\mathcal{C}}^{2}
hyperbolic maps and
[
30
]
for holes in the billiard. There are of course other ways to grasp information about the dependence of the surviving dynamics on the hole, which include the regularity of the topological entropy
[
47
,
13
,
8
]
, escape rates (see, e.g.,
[
31
,
39
,
9
]
and references therein) and Poissonian approximations (see, e.g.,​
[
11
]
and reference therein).

In the present work, we consider Sinai billiards, which are paradigmatic examples of conservative chaotic mechanical systems. The dynamics is given by a point-like particle which moves frictionless in a straight line until it collides elastically with some convex scatterer, or wall, having infinite mass. At collision, the particle conserves its speed and obeys the law that the angle of reflection equals the angle of incidence, as prescribed by momentum and energy conservation. Hyperbolicity is due to the strict convexity of the walls. This guarantees that parallel incoming particles are reflected as a divergent beam after a collision, thus spreading trajectories.

We consider an open system as we place a hole of size
t
in the boundary of the billiard table. The main contribution of this paper is a linear response result: we prove that the function
t\to\mu_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}}(\phi)
is differentiable at
t=0
for any
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
observable
\phi
. To the author’s knowledge, this is the first time linear response is established for an open system. In order to bound the modulus of continuity of
\mu_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}}(\phi)
, one needs to consider convergence under the conditional evolution of an initial measure, which is essentially an indicator function supported on the image of the hole. As the hole shrinks to a point, this initial measure becomes closer and closer to a delta function supported on an unstable curve. Despite being singular, measures supported on unstable curves have in fact nice mixing properties for systems with some hyperbolicity. Indeed, this class of measures, called
standard pairs
, has a long history, tracing back at least to Pesin and Sinai
[
42
]
, and the modern form has been introduced by Dolgopyat
[
34
]
. Standard pairs are often used together with coupling, a technique of probabilistic origin employed to prove exponential decay of correlations, introduced into dynamical systems by Lai-Sang Young
[
51
]
.

Unfortunately, the cases considered in the literature are not sufficient to obtain the needed mixing result. To remedy this, we prove that standard families (which are measures supported on a collection of unstable curves sufficiently long on average) converge exponentially fast to the relevant invariant measure under the conditional evolution of the billiard with the hole. This is a result of independent interest as it expands the class of admissible initial conditions which relax to
\mu_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}}
in the infinite time limit. Together with invariance of standard families, the above tool allows us to prove differentiability.

Linear response lies at the core of nonequilibrium statistical mechanics because of its relation with fluctuation dissipation relations
[
37
,
15
]
. Since the pioneering works of Ruelle in the smooth hyperbolic case
[
44
,
45
]
, linear response has been a very active field in smooth ergodic theory and it has been established in a variety of settings (see, e.g.,
[
33
,
6
]
and recently
[
12
,
2
,
35
]
and references therein). A class of perturbations that somehow deviates from the classical theory is given by discontinuous perturbations and perturbations of discontinuous systems. These emerge in natural physical models
[
16
,
15
,
14
,
26
]
, and are receiving a lot of attention nowadays
[
49
,
50
,
4
,
1
,
41
]
. However, on the mathematical side, the situation is not neat. We know that whenever the perturbation changes the topological class of the unperturbed map, at least for the corresponding theory of 1D expanding systems, there are many ‘typical’ situations in which differentiability fails (see, e.g.,
[
24
,
32
]
and the survey
[
7
]
and references therein).

In
[
12
]
, a mechanism responsible for the differentiability of the SRB measure of certain discontinuous perturbations of 2D conservative chaotic systems has been investigated. We prove here that a similar mechanism also applies when the perturbation is obtained by opening a hole. At an intuitive level, the emergence of a hole represents a departure from conservativity and leads to study inhomogeneities in the mass distribution, which are similar to those produced by contributions from retracting or overlapping branches in deterministic perturbations. Note however that this is just an analogy and the case of holes is different from a classical perturbation. For example, the perturbed operator, which specifies the conditional evolution, is nonlinear.

We remark that our arguments are not billiard-specific - and in fact we believe that they can be extended to different systems with some hyperbolicity and to higher dimensions - but rely significantly on the geometry of the hole. The relevant property is whether one can foliate the image of the hole with long standard pairs. In particular, a circular hole in the phase space of an Anosov map would be out of reach, and we actually expect failure of linear response in that case. Another related but different question is the regularity of
\mu_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}}
for
t>0
, i.e., not in the small hole regime. Both these cases seem to have in common the necessity to introduce very small standard pairs in the picture, thus posing additional obstructions to linear response.

Lastly, we mention that, in the existing literature, the problem of stability for holes or other discontinuous perturbations has also been addressed via the ‘Banach space technology’
[
22
,
26
,
29
,
30
]
. This is a set of powerful techniques, complementary to coupling, based on the abstract perturbation theory developed by Keller and Liverani
[
38
]
. Once this machinery is applied, it yields all at once results about stability of conditionally invariant measures, escape rates and resonances. At the moment, we believe that proving differentiability with Banach space techniques is unfeasible for the systems investigated here. In fact, to obtain linear response, it seems necessary to study the evolution of a single unstable curve. However, as of today and to the author’s knowledge, there are no Banach spaces for general piecewise hyperbolic systems (without a skew-product structure) containing standard pairs.

The paper is structured as follows: in Section
2
, we introduce a uniform class of Sinai billiards with holes, and we state our main result. In Section
3
we recap some basic properties of the billiard map. In Section
4
we state two fundamental results for the conditional dynamics of standard families, namely invariance and ‘conditional’ decay of correlations. These results are then proved in Section
6
. Finally, in Section
5
we prove the main theorem about linear response. Sections
5
and
6
are completely independent of each other.

Throughout the paper
{\mathbb{N}}=\{1,2,...\}
,
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}={\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
,
{\mathbb{T}}^{n}={\mathbb{R}}^{n}/{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
and
c,C>0
are constants which depend only on the billiard table and on the regularity data
\varpi_{1},B_{1},D_{1}
of the initial standard families. We also denote by
C_{\mathrm{cone}}>0
a constant that depends only on the unstable and stable cones. Actually
C_{\mathrm{cone}}
could also be denoted by
C
, but we highlight in this way some dependencies in our arguments. For a vector
v=(v_{1},v_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
|v|=\sqrt{v_{1}^{2}+v_{2}^{2}}
is the Euclidean norm and
B(v,r)
is the open ball of radius
r
centered in
v
. For a set
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
[S]_{r}=\cup_{x\in S}B(x,r)
. For a disjoint union of curves
\Gamma\subset{\mathcal{M}}
, we denote by
|\Gamma|
the sum of their lengths measured in the Euclidean metric. The scatterers are
{\mathcal{D}}=\cup_{i}{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
and the phase space for the billiard is
{\mathcal{M}}=\cup_{i}\partial{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
so that
|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|
is the total perimeter of the scatterers. We denote by
{\mathcal{C}}^{k}({\mathcal{M}})
the set of continuous
k
times differentiable functions on
{\mathcal{M}}
. We write that
P\sim Q
and
P\lesssim Q
as shortcuts for
C^{-1}P\leqslant Q\leqslant CP
and
P\leqslant CQ
respectively.

Acknowledgments:
I am grateful to Nicholas Fleming Vázquez, Jacopo De Simoi and Carlangelo Liverani for many useful conversations. I acknowledge membership in the GNFM/INDAM and the UMI group DinAmicI.

2.
Setting and results

We consider a Sinai billiard in
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
of type A according to the classification in
[
21
]
, namely with bounded horizon and no corners. More precisely, we let
{\mathcal{D}}=\cup_{i}{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\subset{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
be a finite and disjoint union of strictly convex and simply connected regions of
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
. We call these regions scatterers and we assume that each
\partial{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
is a
{\mathcal{C}}^{3}
closed curve. We denote by
{\mathcal{M}}
the
phase space
for the billiard dynamics

{\mathcal{M}}=\cup_{i}\partial{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\times\biggl[-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\biggr],

and by
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
the billiard map. To define
{\mathcal{F}}
, we consider a point-like particle of unit mass and speed that is moving frictionless on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}\setminus{\mathcal{D}}
and when it hits the boundary, it reflects according to the law that the angle of reflection equals the angle of incidence. The map
{\mathcal{F}}
is the Poincaré map corresponding to collisions with the scatterers. Each initial condition is identified by the arc-length coordinate on
\partial{\mathcal{D}}
and the angle of the post-collisional velocity with the normal to the scatterers.

For
x=(r,\varphi)\in{\mathcal{M}}
, we set
x_{n}=(r_{n},\varphi_{n})={\mathcal{F}}^{n}(x)
. We also call
\pi_{r}
and
\pi_{\varphi}
the projections on the first and second coordinate respectively. We assume that the billiard has bounded horizon, i.e., there are no trajectories for the billiard flow that never hit a scatterer. In particular, this implies that the free path between collisions
\tau
is bounded above by some constant (
[
21
, Exercise 2.17]
). We call
\kappa
the curvature of the boundary, which is the modulus of the second derivative of the arc-length parametrization of
\partial{\mathcal{D}}
. Both
\kappa
and
\tau
are functions
{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
. Moreover, the strict convexity assumption implies that
\kappa
is bounded from below. To sum up, as a consequence of our assumptions, there exist
\tau_{\mathrm{min}},\tau_{\mathrm{max}},\kappa_{\mathrm{min}},\kappa_{\mathrm{max}}>0
such that

(2.1)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:bound-table}\tau_{\mathrm{min}}\leqslant\tau\leqslant\tau_{\mathrm{max}}\quad\text{and}\quad\kappa_{\mathrm{min}}\leqslant\kappa\leqslant\kappa_{\mathrm{max}}.

The other bounds are a consequence of the fact that the scatterers are disjoint and their boundaries are
{\mathcal{C}}^{3}
close curves. It is well known
[
21
, Lemma 2.35]
that the billiard map preserves a unique absolutely continuous invariant probability measure
\mu_{0}
, whose density w.r.t. Lebesgue is given by
d\mu_{0}(r,\varphi)=\frac{\cos\varphi}{2|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|}d\varphi dr
.

We now introduce the hole. We fix for the rest of the paper a location
r_{*}\in[0,|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|]
on the boundary of the table and we let
{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
be the hole centered at
r_{*}
of size
t

(2.2)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:def-hole}{\mathcal{H}}_{t}=\biggl[r_{*}-\frac{t|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|}{2},r_{*}+\frac{t|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|}{2}\biggr]\times\biggl[-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\biggr],

in the boundary of some scatterer. The normalization is chosen so that
\mu_{0}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})=t
. We consider the corresponding
open dynamical system
in which particles entering the hole
escape
and we stop considering them. For a small
t_{0}>0
and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we define the open domains

(2.3)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:open-domain}{\mathcal{M}}_{t}={\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}_{t}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}=\cap_{k=0}^{n}{\mathcal{F}}^{-k}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}).

The billiard map with the hole
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
is defined as
{\mathcal{F}}
but restricted to
{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{1}

\begin{split}&{\mathcal{F}}_{t}:{\mathcal{M}}_{t}\cap{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{M}}_{t})\to{\mathcal{M}}_{t},\quad{\mathcal{F}}_{t}={\mathcal{F}}_{|{\mathcal{M}}_{t}\cap{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{M}}_{t})},\quad{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}={\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{|{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}}.\end{split}

This lifts to a map on the set of probability measures given by the conditional probability of survival. For a measure
\nu
with
\nu({\mathcal{M}}_{t}\cap{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}))>0
, we define its conditional evolution
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}\nu
as, for any
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}({\mathcal{M}})
,

(2.4)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:def-push}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}\nu)(\phi)=\frac{\nu(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}\cap{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{M}}_{t})})}{\nu({\mathcal{M}}_{t}\cap{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}))}=\frac{\nu(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{1}})}{\nu({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{1})}.

It is easy to check that iterating (
2.4
), for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and whenever it is well defined,

(2.5)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:iterate-transf-op-hole}({\mathcal{L}}^{n}_{t}\nu)(\phi)=\frac{\nu(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})}{\nu({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}.

Note that
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
preserves the space of probability measures and, in the case
t=0
, it reduces to the usual transfer operator
({\mathcal{L}}_{0}\nu)(\phi)=\nu(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}})
. It is well known (
[
31
, Theorem 2]
,
[
30
, Theorem 8.1]
) that for all
t
small enough there exists a measure
\mu_{t}
such that, for any
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}({\mathcal{M}})
,

(2.6)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:conditional-invariant-measure}\lim_{n\to\infty}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{0})(\phi)=\mu_{t}(\phi).

These measures are conditionally invariant in the sense that
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}\mu_{t}=\mu_{t}
. Recall that
r_{*}
is the center of the hole
{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
. The main result of this paper is the following.

Theorem 2.1
.

For any
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{1}({\mathcal{M}})
the function
t\to\mu_{t}(\phi)
is differentiable at
t=0
and

\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\mu_{t}(\phi)-\mu_{0}(\phi)}{t}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\biggl[\mu_{0}(\phi)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{k}(r_{*},\varphi)\cos\varphi d\varphi\biggr].

The term
\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\frac{\pi}{2}}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{k}(r_{*},\varphi)\cos\varphi d\varphi
can be written as
\mu_{r_{*}}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{k})
where
\mu_{r_{*}}
is the measure obtained by integrating
\cos\varphi
over the vertical line
\{(r_{*},\varphi),\varphi\in[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\}
. As a consequence of Lemma
5.1
and Proposition
4.4
in the case
t=0
, the measures
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}^{k}\mu_{r_{*}}
correspond to standard families, a class of measures defined in Section
4
which are weighted collections of singular measures supported on unstable curves which are not too short on average. It is well known (
[
21
, Theorem 7.31]
or Theorem
4.7
in the case
t=0
) that standard families converge exponentially fast to
\mu_{0}
in the dual of
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
under the dynamics. This in particular implies that the sum on the right-hand side of the equation in Theorem
2.1
is convergent.

3.
Basic properties of the billiard map

Here we recall some well-known properties of
{\mathcal{F}}
and introduce additional notation related to the billiard dynamics. Let
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}:=\partial{\mathcal{M}}=\{\varphi=\pm\pi/2\}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}=\partial{\mathcal{M}}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\partial{\mathcal{M}})
,
{\mathcal{S}}_{-1}=\partial{\mathcal{M}}\cup{\mathcal{F}}(\partial{\mathcal{M}})
be the singularities of the billiard map and its inverse. We define inductively the singularities of the iterates of
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}
respectively as

{\mathcal{S}}_{n+1}={\mathcal{S}}_{n}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{S}}_{n})\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathcal{S}}_{-(n+1)}={\mathcal{S}}_{-n}\cup{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{S}}_{-n}).

Homogeneity strips are a well-known tool used to control distortion. Let
k_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
be a large constant (how large will be specified later on) and for
k\geqslant k_{0}
, we set

(3.1)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:homogeneity}\begin{split}{\mathbb{H}}_{k}={\mathbb{H}}_{k}^{+}\cup{\mathbb{H}}_{k}^{-}&=\{(r,\varphi):\pi/2-k^{-2}<\varphi<\pi/2-(k+1)^{-2}\}\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\cup\{(r,\varphi):-\pi/2+(k+1)^{-2}<\varphi<-\pi/2+k^{-2}\},\\
{\mathbb{H}}_{0}&=\{(r,\varphi):-\pi/2+k_{0}^{2}<\varphi<\pi/2-k_{0}^{2}\}.\end{split}

The boundaries of these strips

\mathbb{S}_{k}=\{(r,\varphi):|\varphi|=\pi/2-k^{-2}\}\cup\{(r,\varphi):|\varphi|=-\pi/2+k^{-2}\},\quad\mathbb{S}=\bigcup_{k\geqslant k_{0}}\mathbb{S}_{k}

define the extended singularity set

{\mathcal{S}}_{n}^{{\mathbb{H}}}={\mathcal{S}}_{n}\cup\bigcup_{k=0}^{n}{\mathcal{F}}^{-k}(\mathbb{S})\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathcal{S}}_{-n}^{{\mathbb{H}}}={\mathcal{S}}_{-n}\cup\bigcup_{k=0}^{n}{\mathcal{F}}^{k}(\mathbb{S}).

We denote by
\partial{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
the discontinuities of the hole, namely the two vertical lines
\{(r_{*}\pm t|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|/2,\varphi):\varphi\in[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\}
. (This set slightly differs from the boundary of
{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
since it does not contains the horizontal boundaries already included in
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
). We introduce the set

\Xi_{n,t}=\bigcup_{k=0}^{n}{\mathcal{F}}^{-k}(\partial{\mathcal{H}}_{t}),

which separates the phase space between trajectories that survive up to time
n
and those that don’t. Finally, we introduce the most general discontinuities

(3.2)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:added-discontinuity-hole}{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}={\mathcal{S}}_{n}\cup\Xi_{n,t}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathcal{S}}^{{\mathbb{H}}}_{n,t}={\mathcal{S}}_{n}^{{\mathbb{H}}}\cup\Xi_{n,t}.

Recalling (
2.3
) for the definition of
{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}
,

(3.3)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:discontinuity-survival}\partial{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}\subseteq\bigcup_{k=0}^{n}{\mathcal{F}}^{-k}(\partial{\mathcal{M}}_{t}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{0})=\bigcup_{k=0}^{n}{\mathcal{F}}^{-k}(\partial{\mathcal{H}}_{t}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{0})={\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}.

As for the hyperbolicity properties, we start by discussing the differential of the map. Denoting by
D_{(r,\varphi)}{\mathcal{F}}
the differential of
{\mathcal{F}}
at the point
(r,\varphi)
, we have (see
[
21
, Equation (2.26)]
)

(3.4)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:differential}\begin{split}D_{(r,\varphi)}{\mathcal{F}}&=\frac{-1}{\cos\varphi_{1}}\begin{pmatrix}\tau\kappa+\cos\varphi&\tau\\
\tau\kappa\kappa_{1}+\kappa\cos\varphi_{1}+\kappa_{1}\cos\varphi&\tau\kappa_{1}+\cos\varphi_{1}\end{pmatrix}\\
&:=\frac{-1}{\cos\varphi_{1}}\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}

Here, we recall that
(r_{1},\varphi_{1})={\mathcal{F}}(r,\varphi)
and we introduced functions
A,B,C
and
D:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
with bounded
L^{\infty}
norm by (
2.1
). In particular,

(3.5)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:bound-differential}\|D_{(r,\varphi)}{\mathcal{F}}\|\leqslant\frac{C}{\cos\varphi_{1}}.

We also introduce the following unstable cone fields,

{\mathcal{C}}^{u}_{x}=\left\{(dr,d\varphi):\kappa\leqslant\frac{d\varphi}{dr}\leqslant\infty\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{u}_{x}=\left\{(dr,d\varphi):\kappa\leqslant\frac{d\varphi}{dr}\leqslant\kappa+\frac{\cos\varphi}{\tau_{-1}}\right\},

and stable counterparts obtained by exchanging
\varphi
with
-\varphi
,

{\mathcal{C}}^{s}_{x}=\left\{(dr,d\varphi):-\infty\leqslant\frac{d\varphi}{dr}\leqslant-\kappa\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{u}_{x}=\left\{(dr,d\varphi):-\kappa-\frac{\cos\varphi}{\tau}\leqslant\frac{d\varphi}{dr}\leqslant-\kappa\right\}.

In the expression above, the subscript
-1
means that the function is evaluated at the preimage of the point. We will sometimes refer to the cone field
{\mathcal{C}}^{u}
as the large unstable cones and to
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{u}
as the small unstable cones, and similarly for the stable cone fields. According to
[
21
, Exercise 4.19]
, the following strong form of cone invariance holds

(3.6)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:cone-invariance}D_{x}{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{C}}_{x}^{u})\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{u}_{{\mathcal{F}}(x)}\quad\text{and}\quad D_{x}{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{C}}_{x}^{s})\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{s}_{{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(x)}.

It is also useful to notice that for any vector
v=(v_{1},v_{2})\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{x}^{u}
or
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{x}^{s}
, we have that
C_{\mathrm{cone}}^{-1}\leqslant|v_{1}/v_{2}|\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}
. We also know (see e.g.
[
21
, Equation (4.19)]
) that there exists
C,\Lambda_{0}>1
, such that, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

(3.7)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:cone-espansion}\inf_{v\in{\mathcal{C}}^{u}_{x}}\frac{\|D{\mathcal{F}}^{n}v\|}{\|v\|}\geqslant C\Lambda_{0}^{n}\quad\text{and}\quad\inf_{v\in{\mathcal{C}}^{s}_{x}}\frac{\|D{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}v\|}{\|v\|}\geqslant C\Lambda_{0}^{n}.

The relations (
3.6
) and (
3.7
) make the billiard map
{\mathcal{F}}
uniformly hyperbolic.
1
1

1

In the sense that the above definition implies the existence of invariant stable and unstable subspaces almost everywhere with uniform contraction and expansion.
We call a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
curve
W\subset{\mathcal{M}}
unstable if the tangent line at any point
x\in W
belongs to the small unstable cone
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{x}^{u}
. By (
3.7
) if
W
is an unstable, resp. stable, curve (or actually just aligned with the large unstable resp.​ stable cone),

(3.8)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:curve-expanding}|{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(W)|\geqslant C\Lambda_{0}^{n}|W|\quad\text{and}\quad|{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}(W)|\geqslant C\Lambda_{0}^{n}|W|.

Recall that
|\cdot|
measures length in the Euclidean norm. The definition of a stable curve is similar.
[
21
, Exercise 4.50]
gives the following bound on the growth of stable and unstable curves.

Lemma 3.1
.

There exists
C>0
such that for any unstable, resp. stable, curve
W
, we have
|{\mathcal{F}}(W)|\leqslant C\sqrt{|W|}
, resp.
|{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(W)|\leqslant C\sqrt{|W|}
.

Recall that
P\sim Q
if
C^{-1}P\leqslant Q\leqslant CP
. The following fact will be used many times.

Lemma 3.2
.

For any unstable curve
W\subset{\mathbb{H}}_{k}
which intersects both sides of
{\mathbb{H}}_{k}
,
\varphi\in\pi_{\varphi}({\mathbb{H}}_{k})
,

|W|\sim k^{-3}\sim\cos^{\frac{3}{2}}\varphi.

Proof.

The first relation follows from the fact that vectors in the small unstable cone have slope bounded from above and below so that the length of any unstable curve in
{\mathbb{H}}_{k}
is comparable with the distance between
\mathbb{S}_{k}
and
\mathbb{S}_{k+1}
. The second relation is a consequence of
\cos\varphi\sim k^{-2}
on
{\mathbb{H}}_{k}
, see (
3.1
).
∎

It is useful to notice that
{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0,t}
is a union of stable curve and
{\mathcal{S}}_{-n,t}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0,t}
is a union of unstable curves. Indeed, for
t=0
the statement follows by
[
21
, Proposition 4.41]
and for
t>0
we have that
\partial{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
are vertical lines and are aligned to both the large stable and the large unstable cone field. Then the statement follows by cone invariance (
3.6
). As such,
{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0,t}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{-n,t}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0,t}
are transversal to unstable and stable curves respectively, uniformly in the location. The next property is often referred to as
continuation of singularity lines
.

Lemma 3.3
.

For any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, every curve
S\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0,t}
is part of some monotonic continuous and piece-wise
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
curve
\widetilde{S}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0,t}
which terminates on
\partial{\mathcal{M}}
.

In the case
t=0
this is
[
21
, Proposition 4.47]
. For
t>0
, we note that
{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}
coincides with the singularities of a billiard with a couple of ‘fake corners’ placed at the boundary of the hole
\partial\pi_{r}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})
. These billiards can be regarded as billiards with corners and
[
21
, Proposition 4.47]
holds as well, proving the statement. We will also use the following simple fact.

Lemma 3.4
.

For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}
is a finite union of
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
components whose number is bounded uniformly in
t
.

Proof.

For
n=0
the statement holds. Suppose that the statement is true for some
n
so that
{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}
is a finite union of at most
M_{n}>0
curves, uniformly in
t
. Notice that
{\mathcal{S}}_{n+1,t}={\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{S}}_{n,t})
. The set
{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}
consists of curves which are transversal to the small unstable cone field so that each of them intersects
{\mathcal{S}}_{-1}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
in at most one point. Thus, the preimage under
{\mathcal{F}}
of any of these curves is composed of at most two components and
{\mathcal{S}}_{n+1,t}
is composed of at most
2M_{n}+M_{n}
curves, independently of
t
.
∎

4.
Standard Families

To prove linear response, we need a preliminary result about convergence to equilibrium for the open billiard dynamics starting from a certain class of initial measures. Recall that a smooth curve
W\subset{\mathcal{M}}
is called unstable if the tangent space
{\mathcal{T}}_{x}W
at
x\in W
is aligned with
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{u}_{x}
for any
x\in W
. Since the vectors in the small unstable cone have bounded slope,
W
is the graph of a function
\varphi_{W}
. For
D>0
and
\delta_{*}\leqslant 1
, we denote by
\mathcal{W}(D)
the set of admissible unstable curves,

(4.1)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:graph-sp-def}\begin{split}\mathcal{W}(D)=\biggl\{W=\varphi_{W}(I):\text{ }|W|\leqslant\delta_{*},\quad\varphi_{W}&\in{\mathcal{C}}^{2}\biggl(I,\biggl[-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\biggr]\biggr),\\
&{\mathcal{T}}_{x}W\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{x}^{u}\text{ }\forall x\in W,\quad|\varphi_{W}^{\prime\prime}|\leqslant D\biggr\}.\\
\end{split}

In the definition above,
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}
is an interval which coincides with the
r
-projection of
W
. Whenever we want to indicate the interval corresponding to some particular
W\in\mathcal{W}(D)
, we write

I_{W}:=\{r\in[0,|\Gamma|]:(r,\varphi_{W}(r))\in W\}=\pi_{r}(W).

The curves in (
4.1
) are everywhere aligned with the unstable cone field and are graphs of
{\mathcal{C}}^{2}
functions.
Moreover, their size is at most
\delta_{*}
. The parameter
\delta_{*}
is to be considered fixed (even if its specific value will be specified later on), and so we do not track this dependence in the notation. We now equip the curves in
\mathcal{W}
with a regular density. For
\varpi>0
, set

(4.2)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:cones-def}\begin{split}&{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi)=\biggl\{\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(I,{\mathbb{R}}):\quad\frac{\rho(x)}{\rho(y)}\leqslant e^{\varpi|x-y|^{\frac{1}{3}}}\text{ }\forall x,y\in I,\quad\rho>0,\text{ }\int_{I}\rho=1\biggr\},\end{split}

and
{\mathcal{C}}=\cup_{\varpi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi)
. Quite often we will use the following simple estimate.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
W\in\mathcal{W}(D)
and
\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi)
defined on
I_{W}
. We have, for some
C_{\mathrm{cone}}>0
,

\frac{e^{-\varpi}}{|W|}\leqslant\rho\leqslant\frac{C_{\mathrm{cone}}e^{\varpi}}{|W|}.

Proof.

By the mean value theorem there exists
\overline{x}\in I_{W}
such that
\rho(\overline{x})=1/|I_{W}|
. Since
{\mathcal{T}}_{x}W\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{x}^{u}
, we have that
|I_{W}|\leqslant|W|\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}|I_{W}|
. The bound then follows by the definition of the cone and the fact that
|I_{W}|\leqslant|W|\leqslant\delta_{*}\leqslant 1
.
∎

We call
standard pair
the pair
\ell=(W,\rho)
, where
W\in\mathcal{W}(D)
and
\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}
is defined on
I_{W}
. For a countable set of indices
{\mathcal{J}}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}
, we call the collection
{\mathcal{G}}=\{(p_{j},\ell_{j})\}_{j\in{\mathcal{J}}}\equiv\{(p_{j},W_{j},\rho_{j})\}_{j\in{\mathcal{J}}}
a standard family, where
p_{j}\geqslant 0
,
\sum_{j}p_{j}=1
and
\ell_{j}
are standard pairs. We will often omit the index set
{\mathcal{J}}
. Any standard pair
\ell
induces a measure on
{\mathcal{M}}
by

\mu_{\ell}(\phi)=\int_{I_{W}}\rho(r)\phi(r,\varphi_{W}(r))dr,\quad\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}({\mathcal{M}}).

Similarly, a standard family induces a measure given by the convex combination of the measures of the associated standard pairs,

\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}(\phi)=\sum_{j}p_{j}\mu_{\ell_{j}}(\phi),\quad\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}({\mathcal{M}}).

We need to keep track of how small the average curve is in a standard family. To this aim, we introduce the
measure of the boundary
(or just
boundary
2
2

2

See
[
21
, Exercise 7.14]
for an explanation of the name.
)
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}})
of the standard family
{\mathcal{G}}=\{(p_{j},W_{j},\rho_{j})\}
,

(4.3)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:boundary-def}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}})=\sum_{j}p_{j}\frac{1}{|W_{j}|}.

It turns out that when evolving standard families it is sufficient to keep track of only a few parameters: the value of
\mathcal{Z}
in (
4.3
) and
\varpi
in (
4.2
). The first value tells how regular the support of
\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}
is, while the second tells how regular the densities are. We will sometimes refer to these values as the regularity of the standard family. For technical reasons, we also need to record the bound on the second derivative of the graphs
\varphi_{W}
in
\mathcal{W}
.

Definition 4.2
.

We say that a standard family
{\mathcal{G}}=\{\bigl(p_{j},W_{j},\rho_{j})\}_{j\in{\mathcal{J}}}
is a
(\varpi,B,D)
-standard family for some
\varpi,B,D>0
if
\rho_{j}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi)
,
W_{j}\in\mathcal{W}(D)
for all
j\in{\mathcal{J}}
and
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}})\leqslant B
.

Because of transversality, standard families consisting of long unstable curves cannot concentrate too much mass inside the hole.

Lemma 4.3
.

There exists
C>0
such that for any
(\varpi,B,D)
-standard family
{\mathcal{G}}
,
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\leqslant Ce^{\varpi}Bt.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=\{(p_{j},W_{j},\rho_{j})\}
and note that
|\pi_{r}(W_{j}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{t})|\leqslant|\pi_{r}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})|=t|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|
. Moreover, since
\rho_{j}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi)
and
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}})\leqslant B
, using Lemma
4.1
,

\begin{split}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})=\sum_{j}p_{j}\int_{I_{W_{j}}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}}(r,\varphi_{W_{j}}(r))&\rho_{j}(r)dr=\sum_{j}p_{j}\int_{\pi_{r}(W_{j}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{t})}\!\!\!\rho_{j}\\
&\leqslant|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|C_{\mathrm{cone}}e^{\varpi}\sum_{j}p_{j}\frac{t}{|W_{j}|}\leqslant Ce^{\varpi}Bt.\end{split}

∎

We now state two results on standard families that will be proved in Section
6
. These are essentially all we need to know for our application to linear response. The first result is about invariance of standard families.

Proposition 4.4
.

For any
\varpi_{1},B_{1},D_{1}>0
there exist
\varpi_{2},B_{2},D_{2}>0
and
t_{0}>0
such that the following happens. For any
(\varpi_{1},B_{1},D_{1})
-standard family
{\mathcal{G}}
, any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, and any
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, there exists another
(\varpi_{2},B_{2},D_{2})
-standard family
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
such that, for any
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}({\mathcal{M}})
,

({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}})(\phi)=\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}}(\phi).

We now fix throughout Sections
4
and
5
some big enough value of
\varpi_{1},B_{1}
and
D_{1}
, and we let
\varpi_{2}
,
B_{2}
and
D_{2}
be given by Proposition
4.4
. In particular, we consider
\varpi_{1}\geqslant\varpi_{*}
,
B_{1}\geqslant B_{*}
and
D_{1}\geqslant D_{*}
where
\varpi_{*},B_{*},D_{*}>0
are given by Lemma
5.1
. We call any
(\varpi_{1},B_{1},D_{1})
-standard family a
initial regular standard family
and any
(\varpi_{2},B_{2},D_{2})
-standard family a
regular standard family
. With this terminology, Proposition
4.4
tells that the conditional evolution of
\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}
for an initial regular standard family
{\mathcal{G}}
is a measure associated with a regular standard family.

Remark 4.5
.

The constants
B_{2},\varpi_{2},D_{2}
in Proposition
4.4
depend only on
B_{1},\varpi_{1},D_{1}
and the billiard table. Hence, they can be incorporated in the constant
C
. See the discussion about the general constant
C
at the end of Section
1
.

We don’t really need to keep track of the value of the second derivative
\varphi_{W}^{\prime\prime}
, but we need only to have a uniform bound throughout the argument. Hence, we will often omit to record the data about
|\varphi_{W}^{\prime\prime}|
. In particular, we say that
{\mathcal{G}}
is a
(\varpi,B)
-standard family if the conditions on the density and boundary are satisfied and
W_{j}\in\Sigma(D_{2})
for all
j
. Before presenting the second result, we add an observation for later, which is a consequence of Proposition
4.4
. It cannot happen that for some iterate all the mass of a regular standard family ends up in the hole.

Lemma 4.6
.

There exist
C,t_{0}>0
such that, for any initial regular standard family
{\mathcal{G}}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, we have
\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})\geqslant(1-Ct)^{n}
.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
be a regular standard family (a
(\varpi_{2},B_{2},D_{2})
-standard family, as described after Proposition
4.4
). Observe that

\mu_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}))\leqslant\mu_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})+\mu_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}))=\mu_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})+({\mathcal{L}}_{0}\mu_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})({\mathcal{H}}_{t}),

where
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
is given by (
2.4
) with
t=0
. We can then apply Proposition
4.4
in the case
t=0
for initial standard families that are
(\varpi_{2},B_{2},D_{2})
and we have that
({\mathcal{L}}_{0}\mu_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}})
is a
(\varpi_{3},B_{3},D_{3})
-standard family for some
\varpi_{3},B_{3},D_{3}>0
depending only on
\varpi_{2},B_{2},D_{2}
and the billiard table. Therefore, by Remark
4.5
, the constants
\varpi_{2},\varpi_{3},B_{2},B_{3},D_{2}
and
D_{3}
can be incorporated in the generic constant
C
as well. Hence, by Lemma
4.3
and the equation above, there exists
C>0
such that for any regular standard family
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
,

(4.4)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:hole-plus-preimage}\mu_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}))\leqslant Ce^{\varpi_{2}}B_{2}t+Ce^{\varpi_{3}}B_{3}t\leqslant Ct.

In other words, there exists
C>0
such that for any regular standard family
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

(4.5)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:mass-hole-first-iteration}\mu_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{1})=1-\mu_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}))\geqslant 1-Ct.

In particular, applying (
4.5
) to the initial regular standard family
{\mathcal{G}}
of the statement proves the case
n=1
. For any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

(4.6)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:induction-hole-mass-inside}\begin{split}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n+1})=\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}\cap{\mathcal{F}}^{-n-1}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}))&=\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}})({\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{M}}_{t})).\end{split}

Let
t_{0}>0
be such that Proposition
4.4
applies and assume that the statement holds for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. By the mentioned proposition, for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, there exists a regular standard family
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
such that
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}=\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}
. Therefore, by (
4.5
) in the case
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}={\mathcal{G}}_{n}
, equation (
4.6
) and the inductive hypothesis,

\begin{split}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n+1})&=\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}({\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}))\geqslant\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{n}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{1})\geqslant(1-Ct)^{n+1}.\end{split}

This concludes the proof of the statement.
∎

The second result tells that regular initial standard families converge exponentially fast to the physically relevant conditionally invariant measure
\mu_{t}
under the action of the renormalized push-forward.

Theorem 4.7
.

There exist
t_{0}>0
,
C>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
such that for any two initial regular standard families
{\mathcal{G}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{G}}_{2}
for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{1}({\mathcal{M}})
and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{1}})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{2}})(\phi)|\leqslant C\|\phi\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}\gamma^{n}.

The above result, together with the fact that
\mu_{0}
is well approximated by standard families (see Lemma
5.8
), implies exponential convergence of standard families under the conditional evolution to the unique physically relevant measure
\mu_{t}
.

Corollary 4.8
.

There exist
t_{0}>0
,
C>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
such that for any initial regular standard family
{\mathcal{G}}
, for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{1}({\mathcal{M}})
,

|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}})(\phi)-\mu_{t}(\phi)|\leqslant C\|\phi\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}\gamma^{n}.

The proof of this Corollary is in the next section.

5.
Linear Response

In this section we use the results of Section
4
to prove Theorem
2.1
. We start by proving that the push-forward of certain measures supported on vertical lines are standard families.

Lemma 5.1
.

There exist
\varpi_{*},B_{*},D_{*}>0
such that, for any
r\in[0,|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|]
, there exists a
(\varpi_{*},B_{*},D_{*})
-standard family
{\mathcal{G}}_{r}
such that, for any
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}({\mathcal{M}})
,

\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}(r,\varphi)\cos\varphi d\varphi=\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{r}}(\phi).

Proof.

Denote by
l_{r}
the vertical line

l_{r}=\{r\}\times\biggl[-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\biggr].

The support of a candidate standard family
\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{r}}
have to coincide with
{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})
. Recalling that
(r_{1}(r,\varphi),\varphi_{1}(r,\varphi))={\mathcal{F}}(r,\varphi)
and using (
3.4
) for the differential and (
2.1
), we obtain

(5.1)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:implicit-function-theorem}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}r_{1}(r,\varphi)=-\frac{\tau}{\cos\varphi_{1}}(r,\varphi)\leqslant-\tau_{\mathrm{min}}<0.

Hence, the function
\varphi\to r_{1}(r,\varphi)
is strictly decreasing and the inverse
\overline{\varphi}_{r}:\pi_{r}({\mathcal{F}}(l_{r}))\to[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
is well defined and it is such that
r_{1}(r,\overline{\varphi}_{r}(r_{1}))=r_{1}
. Moreover,

(5.2)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:derivative-inverse}\frac{d\overline{\varphi}_{r}}{dr_{1}}(r_{1})=-\frac{\cos\varphi_{1}}{\tau}(r,\overline{\varphi}_{r}(r_{1})).

For
j,k\in\{0\}\cup\{n\geqslant k_{0}\}
,
s_{1},s_{2}\in\{+,-\}
, we denote by
W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}
the maximal connected components of

(5.3)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:1-homogeneous-push-vertical}{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})\cap{\mathbb{H}}_{k}^{s_{1}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}({\mathbb{H}}_{j}^{s_{2}}).

Recall
{\mathbb{H}}_{k}^{\pm}
from (
3.1
). These are the subsets of
{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})
which belong to just one homogeneity strip and such that their preimage also belongs to just one homogeneity strip. Set
\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}:\pi_{r}({\mathcal{F}}(l_{r}))\to[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
,

(5.4)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:push-graph}\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{1})=\varphi_{1}(r,\overline{\varphi}_{r}(r_{1})).

This is the graph of
{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})
. By (
5.2
) and a change of variable, and partitioning
\pi_{r}({\mathcal{F}}(l_{r}))
into homogeneous components,

(5.5)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:change-variable-vertical}\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}&\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}(r,\varphi)\cos\varphi d\varphi=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\pi_{r}({\mathcal{F}}(l_{r}))}\phi(r_{1},\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{1}))\cos\overline{\varphi}_{r}(r_{1})\frac{\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{1})}{\tau}dr_{1}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}\int_{I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}}\phi(r_{1},\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{1}))\cos\overline{\varphi}_{r}(r_{1})\frac{\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{1})}{\tau}dr_{1}.\end{split}

Here the minus sign in (
5.1
) cancels with the orientation of
\pi_{r}({\mathcal{F}}(l_{r}))
. Setting

(5.6)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:density-push-vertical}\begin{split}p_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}&=\int_{I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}}\rho,\quad\rho=\frac{\cos\overline{\varphi}_{r}\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}}{2\tau},\\
&{\mathcal{G}}_{r}=\{(p_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}},W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}},(\rho{\mathbbm{1}}_{I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}})/p_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}})\}_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}},\end{split}

Equation (
5.5
) can be written in the form of a measure associated with the standard family
{\mathcal{G}}_{r}
. However, we need to check the properties of the graph
\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}
, the density
\rho
and the boundary
\mathcal{Z}
. By (
3.6
) and because the vertical direction belongs to
{\mathcal{C}}^{u}
, we have that
{\mathcal{T}}_{x}W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{x}^{u}
. Moreover, recalling (
3.4
) and (
5.2
), we have, on
I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}
,

(5.7)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:derivativeG-push-vertical}\begin{split}\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}^{\prime}(r_{1})=\frac{d}{d\varphi}\varphi_{1}(r,\cdot)_{|\overline{\varphi}_{r}(r_{1})}\frac{d\overline{\varphi}_{r}}{dr_{1}}(r_{1})&=-\frac{\tau\kappa_{1}+\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{1})}{\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{1})}\biggl(-\frac{\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{1})}{\tau}\biggr)\\
&=\frac{\tau\kappa_{1}+\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{1})}{\tau}.\end{split}

Here
\tau=\tau(r,\overline{\varphi}_{r}(r_{1}))
and
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{1}(r_{1})
are functions of
r_{1}
only. In particular, calling
(\overline{x},\overline{y})
the parametrization of
\partial{\mathcal{D}}
w.r.t.​ the arc-length,

(5.8)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:tau-regularity}\tau(r,\overline{\varphi}_{r}(r_{1}))=\sqrt{\bigl(\overline{x}(r)-\overline{x}(r_{1})\bigr)^{2}+\bigl(\overline{y}(r)-\overline{y}(r_{1})\bigr)^{2}}.

Hence, by the
{\mathcal{C}}^{3}
assumption on
\partial{\mathcal{D}}
and the fact that
\tau\geqslant\tau_{\mathrm{min}}>0
, we have that the function
r_{1}\to\tau(r,\overline{\varphi}_{r}(r_{1}))
is uniformly
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
in the domain
I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}
since
W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}\subset{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-1}
. The same holds for
\kappa
because of the regularity of
\partial{\mathcal{D}}
. By differentiating (
5.7
) once more with respect to
r_{1}
we obtain

\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{\tau^{\prime}\kappa_{1}+\tau\kappa_{1}^{\prime}-\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}^{\prime}\sin\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}}{\tau}-\frac{\tau\kappa_{1}+\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}}{\tau^{2}}\tau^{\prime}.

By (
5.7
) (or simply noticing that
{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})
belongs to the small unstable cone), we have that
|\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}^{\prime}|
is bounded. Therefore, by the discussion on the regularity of
\tau
and
\kappa
and the equation above, there exists
D_{*}>0
big enough such that
|\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}^{\prime\prime}|\leqslant D_{*}
. In order to dilute the technical part of the argument, we postpone the proof that
\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi_{*})
for some
\varpi_{*}>0
to Lemma
5.2
right below. It remains to estimate the boundary
\mathcal{Z}
. Recalling the definition (
5.3
) of
W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}
and by Lemma
3.2
, for all
j,k,s_{1},s_{2}
,

(5.9)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:estimates-cosines-0}\begin{split}&\max_{I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}}\cos\overline{\varphi}_{r}\sim j^{-2}\quad\text{and}\quad\max_{I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}}\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}\sim k^{-2}.\end{split}

In the first equation we used that
{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}})\subset{\mathbb{H}}_{j}^{s_{2}}
and in the second that
W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}\subset{\mathbb{H}}_{k}^{s_{1}}
.
Hence, by (
5.6
) and (
5.9
), we have that

p_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}=\int_{I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}}\frac{\cos\overline{\varphi}_{r}\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}}{2\tau}\leqslant C\frac{|I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}|}{j^{2}k^{2}}\leqslant C\frac{|W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}|}{j^{2}k^{2}}.

Recalling that
s_{1},s_{2}\in\{+,-\}
, there exists
B_{*}>0
such that

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}}_{r})=\sum_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}p_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}\frac{1}{|W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}|}\leqslant C\sum_{j,k}\frac{1}{j^{2}k^{2}}\leqslant B_{*}.\end{split}

This concludes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\rho
be defined by (
5.6
) and
W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}
by (
5.3
). There exists
\varpi_{*}>0
such that for any
r_{a},r_{b}\in I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}
,

\frac{\rho(r_{a})}{\rho(r_{b})}\leqslant e^{\varpi_{*}|r_{a}-r_{b}|^{\frac{1}{3}}}.

Proof.

Recall (
5.4
) and the discussion after (
5.1
) for the definition of
\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}
and
\overline{\varphi}_{r}
. By (
3.5
), for some
c>0
,

|{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}})|\geqslant c|W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}|\min_{I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}}\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}

Moreover, by definition
W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}
is a subset of
{\mathbb{H}}_{k}^{s_{1}}
so that by Lemma
3.2
, for every
r_{1}\in I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}
, we have that
\cos\overline{\varphi}_{r}\sim|{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}})|^{2/3}
and, for some
C>0
,

\begin{split}\frac{\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{1})}{\cos\overline{\varphi}_{r}(r_{1})}&\leqslant C\frac{\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{1})}{|{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}})|^{\frac{2}{3}}}\leqslant C\frac{\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{1})}{|W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}|^{\frac{2}{3}}\min_{I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}}\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{1})^{\frac{2}{3}}}\\
&\leqslant C\frac{1}{|W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}|^{\frac{2}{3}}}\leqslant CC_{\mathrm{cone}}\frac{1}{|I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}|^{\frac{2}{3}}}.\end{split}

Therefore, using the lower bound on
\tau
and (
5.2
), for some
C>0
and any
r_{a},r_{b}\in I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}
,

\begin{split}|\ln\cos\overline{\varphi}_{r}(r_{a})-\ln\cos\overline{\varphi}_{r}(r_{b})|&\leqslant\int_{r_{a}}^{r_{b}}\biggl|\frac{\sin\overline{\varphi}_{r}(r_{1})}{\cos\overline{\varphi}_{r}(r_{1})}\frac{\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{1})}{\tau}\biggr|dr_{1}\leqslant\frac{C|r_{b}-r_{a}|}{|I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}|^{\frac{2}{3}}}\leqslant C|r_{b}-r_{a}|^{\frac{1}{3}}.\end{split}

Since
\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}\sim|W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}|^{2/3}\sim|I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}|^{2/3}
, a similar computation yields, for some
C>0
,

\begin{split}|\ln\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{a})&-\ln\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{b})|\leqslant\int_{r_{a}}^{r_{b}}\biggl|\frac{\sin\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{1})}{\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}(r_{1})}\biggr||\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})}^{\prime}(r_{1})|dr_{1}\leqslant C|r_{b}-r_{a}|^{\frac{1}{3}}.\end{split}

Finally, using the fact that
\tau
is uniformly
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
as a function of
r_{1}
on
I_{W_{j,k,s_{1},s_{2}}}
, as discussed after (
5.8
), for some
C>0
,

|\ln\tau(r_{a})-\ln\tau(r_{b})|\leqslant\int_{r_{b}}^{r_{a}}\biggl|\frac{\tau^{\prime}(r_{1})}{\tau(r_{1})}\biggr|dr_{1}\leqslant C|r_{a}-r_{b}|.

Since
\rho=(\cos\overline{\varphi}_{r}\cos\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}(l_{r})})/(2\tau)
, the previous three equations prove the Lemma.
∎

Recalling the discussion after Proposition
4.4
, we fix
\varpi_{1}>\varpi_{*}
,
B_{1}>B_{*}
and
D_{1}>D_{*}
so that, according to Lemma
5.1
, the measures

\phi\to\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}(r,\varphi)\cos\varphi d\varphi,

correspond to initial regular standard families for any
r
. Working with regular standard families is almost sufficient for our calculations, but not really. We need to ‘pass to the limit’ with the following slightly larger class of measures
{\mathcal{M}}_{\omega,B,D}
.

Definition 5.3
.

We denote by
{\mathcal{M}}_{\omega,B,D}
, for
\omega,B,D>0
, the weak-
\star
closure of the set of measures corresponding to
(\omega,B,D)
-standard families. We say that
\mu
is a initial regular measure if
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\varpi_{1},B_{1},D_{1}}
and that
\mu
is a regular measure if
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\varpi_{2},B_{2},D_{2}}
.

In the next Lemma we show that
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\varpi,B,D}
has a representation in terms of standard families also when it acts on discontinuous functions whose discontinuities are allowed only on
{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}
. This result uses transversality between unstable curves and
{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}
and the fact that the family has bounded
\mathcal{Z}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\omega,B,D}
and
\{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}\}_{k}
be any sequence of
(\omega,B,D)
-standard families such that
\mu=\lim_{k}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}
. For any bounded function
h
, discontinuous at most on
{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, we have that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}(h)=\mu(h)
.

Proof.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, let
h_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}({\mathcal{M}})
be such that
h_{\varepsilon}=h
on
{\mathcal{M}}\setminus[{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}]_{\varepsilon}
and
\|h_{\varepsilon}\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{0}}\leqslant\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}
. Set also
{\mathcal{G}}_{k}=\{(p_{j}^{k},W_{j}^{k},\rho_{j}^{k})\}
. Recall from the discussion after Lemma
3.2
that
{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0,t}
is a union of stable curves. Therefore, using also Lemma
3.4
, for any
n
, the set
{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}
consists of a finite union of curves transversal to the unstable cone whose number and transversality is uniform in
t
. Hence, there exists
C_{n}>0
such that, for all
k
,
j
,
t\in[0,t_{0}]
and
\varepsilon>0
,

(5.10)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:transversality-unstable-discontinuities-unif-t}|\pi_{r}(W_{j}^{k}\cap[{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}]_{\varepsilon})|\leqslant|W_{j}^{k}\cap[{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}]_{\varepsilon}|\leqslant C_{n}\varepsilon.

By definition of
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\omega,B,D}
, we have that
\rho_{j}^{k}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi)
. Hence, by (
5.10
), Lemma
4.1
and the fact that
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}}_{k})\leqslant B
, for some
C_{\mathrm{cone}}>0
,

\begin{split}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}([{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}]_{\varepsilon})&=\sum_{j}p_{j}^{k}\int_{\pi_{r}(W_{j}^{k}\cap[{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}]_{\varepsilon})}\rho_{j}^{k}\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}e^{\varpi}\sum_{j}p^{k}_{j}\frac{C_{n}\varepsilon}{|W_{j}^{k}|}\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}e^{\varpi}C_{n}B\varepsilon.\end{split}

Therefore, by definition of
h_{\varepsilon}
, for each
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\varepsilon>0
,

(5.11)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Gk-todiscontinuous}|\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}(|h-h_{\varepsilon}|)|\leqslant\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}([{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}]_{\varepsilon})\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}e^{\varpi}C_{n}B\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}\varepsilon.

By Lusin’s theorem, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exist a set
L_{\varepsilon}
and a continuous function
\widetilde{h}_{\varepsilon}
such that
h_{\varepsilon}-h=\widetilde{h}_{\varepsilon}
on
L_{\varepsilon}
and
\mu({\mathcal{M}}\setminus L_{\varepsilon})\leqslant\varepsilon
. We can also require that
\|\widetilde{h}_{\varepsilon}\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{0}}\leqslant\|h-h_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}
. Therefore,

\begin{split}\mu(h_{\varepsilon})-\mu(h)=\mu(h_{\varepsilon}-h)&=\mu(\widetilde{h}_{\varepsilon})+\mu((\widetilde{h}_{\varepsilon}-h_{\varepsilon}-h){\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}\setminus L_{\varepsilon}})\\
&=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}(\widetilde{h}_{\varepsilon})+\mu((\widetilde{h}_{\varepsilon}-h_{\varepsilon}-h){\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}\setminus L_{\varepsilon}}).\end{split}

By the equation above and (
5.11
), for any
\varepsilon>0

(5.12)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:muheps}|\mu(h_{\varepsilon})-\mu(h)|\leqslant\sup_{k}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}(|h-h_{\varepsilon}|)+\mu({\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}\setminus L_{\varepsilon}})\|\widetilde{h}_{\varepsilon}-h_{\varepsilon}-h\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant(C_{\mathrm{cone}}e^{\varpi}C_{n}B+4)\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}\varepsilon.

Moreover, since
h_{\varepsilon}
is a continuous function,
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}(h_{\varepsilon})=\mu(h_{\varepsilon})
. Therefore, by (
5.11
) and (
5.12
), for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
k
big enough such that

\begin{split}|\mu(h)-\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}(h)|&\leqslant|\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}(h)-\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}(h_{\varepsilon})|+|\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}(h_{\varepsilon})-\mu(h_{\varepsilon})|+|\mu(h_{\varepsilon})-\mu(h)|\\
&\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}e^{\varpi}C_{n}B\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}\varepsilon+\varepsilon+(C_{\mathrm{cone}}e^{\varpi}C_{n}B+4)\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}\varepsilon.\end{split}

The arbitrariness of
\varepsilon
concludes the proof of the statement.
∎

Recall that initial regular measures were introduced by Definition
5.3
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\mu
be a initial regular measure and
\{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}\}_{k}
be a sequence of initial regular standard families such that
\mu=\lim_{k}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}
. There exists
t_{0}>0
such that, for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

\lim_{k\to\infty}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}})=({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu).

Proof.

First notice that by Lemma
4.6
there exists
t_{0}
small enough such that
\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})\geqslant(1/2)^{n}
for any
k
and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
. By (
3.3
) the function
{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}}
is discontinuous at most on
{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}
so that by Lemma
5.4
we have that
\mu({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})\geqslant(1/2)^{n}
as well. Therefore, for any
t\in[0,t_{0}]
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, both measures
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu
and
{\mathcal{L}}^{n}_{t}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}
are well defined.
Recalling the expression (
2.5
) for the iterates
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}
, for any
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}({\mathcal{M}})
,

({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu)(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}})(\phi)=\frac{\mu(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})-\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})\mu({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}{\mu({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
. By (
3.3
),
\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}}
is discontinuous at most on
{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}
. Hence, by Lemma
5.4
, there exists
k
big enough such that

|\mu(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})-\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})|\leqslant\varepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad|\mu({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})-\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})|\leqslant\varepsilon.

Therefore, using the lower bounds on
\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t,n})
and
\mu({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})

\begin{split}|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu)(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}})(\phi)|&\leqslant\frac{1}{\mu({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}\bigl(|\mu(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})-\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})|\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt|\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})|+|\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})||\mu({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})-\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})|\bigr)\\
&\leqslant 4^{n}(\varepsilon+\|\phi\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{0}}\varepsilon).\end{split}

By the arbitrariness of
\varepsilon
, we conclude the proof.
∎

In the next Lemma we ‘lift’ invariance for standard families to the bigger class
{\mathcal{M}}_{\varpi,B,D}
.

Lemma 5.6
.

There exists
t_{0}>0
such that, for any initial regular measure
\mu
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, we have that
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu
is a regular measure.

Proof.

By Definition
5.3
of initial regular measure
\mu
there exists a sequence
\{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}\}
of initial regular standard families such that
\mu=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}
. By Lemma
5.5
, for
t_{0}>0
small enough and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, we have that
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}
for any
n
. On the other hand, by Proposition
4.4
and because
{\mathcal{G}}_{k}
are initial regular, there exist regular standard families
{\mathcal{G}}_{n,k}^{\prime}
such that
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}=\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k,n}^{\prime}}
. Hence,
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{n,k}^{\prime}}
, showing that
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu
are regular measures.
∎

The decay of correlations for initial regular standard families also carries over to initial regular measures.

Lemma 5.7
.

There exists
t_{0}>0
,
C>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
such that for any two initial regular measure
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
,
t\in[0,t_{0}]
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, for any
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{1}({\mathcal{M}})
,

\bigl|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{1})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{2})(\phi)\bigr|\leqslant C\|\phi\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}\gamma^{n}.

Proof.

Let
C>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
be given by Theorem
4.7
and
t_{0}>0
small enough so that Theorem
4.7
and Lemma
5.5
apply, and take
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{1}({\mathcal{M}})
. By Lemma
5.5
and because
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are initial regular measures, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exist two initial regular standard families
{\mathcal{G}}_{1,\varepsilon}
and
{\mathcal{G}}_{2,\varepsilon}
such that,

|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{\iota})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota,\varepsilon}})(\phi)|\leqslant\varepsilon\quad\text{for }\iota\in\{1,2\}.

Hence, by Theorem
4.7
,

\begin{split}|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{1})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{2})(\phi)|&\leqslant|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{1,\varepsilon}})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{2,\varepsilon}})(\phi)|+|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{1})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{1,\varepsilon}})(\phi)|\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{2})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{2,\varepsilon}})(\phi)|\\
&\leqslant C\|\phi\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}\gamma^{n}+2\varepsilon,\end{split}

and, by the arbitrariness of
\varepsilon
, we conclude the proof of the Lemma.
∎

For any
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, define the measure
{\mathcal{D}}_{t}
as, for
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}({\mathcal{M}})
,

(5.13)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:survival-and-hole-measures}{\mathcal{D}}_{t}(\phi)=\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}))}=\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})})}{t}=\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}})}{t}.

Recall that
\mu_{0}
is the SRB probability measure for the billiard map
{\mathcal{F}}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

For any
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, the measures
{\mathcal{D}}_{t}
and
\mu_{0}
are initial regular measures.

Proof.

Let
t\in[0,t_{0}]
. We first show that
{\mathcal{D}}_{t}
is an initial regular measure. Recall that
r_{*}\in[0,|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|]
is the arc-length coordinate of the center of the hole
{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
. For any
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
j\in\{0,1,...,N-1\}
, let
r_{j}=r_{*}-\frac{t|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|}{2}+j\frac{t|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|}{N}
be a evenly spread grid in
\pi_{r}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})
and set

(5.14)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:discrete-approx}{\mathcal{D}}_{t,N}(\phi)=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{2N}\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}(r_{j},\varphi)\cos\varphi d\varphi.

The measures
{\mathcal{D}}_{t,N}
are discrete - vertical lines - approximations of
{\mathcal{D}}_{t}
as it can be seen by the last expression for
{\mathcal{D}}_{t}
in (
5.13
).
By Lemma
5.1
, there exist initial regular standard families
{\mathcal{G}}_{r_{j}}
such that,

{\mathcal{D}}_{t,N}=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{N}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{r_{j}}}.

Since the regularity of standard families is preserved by convex combinations, we have that
{\mathcal{D}}_{t,N}=\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{t,N}}
for some initial regular standard family
{\mathcal{G}}_{t,N}
. Hence, in order to prove that
{\mathcal{D}}_{t}
is an initial regular measure, it remains to prove that
\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathcal{D}}_{t,N}={\mathcal{D}}_{t}
on continuous functions. To this aim, consider the measures, for
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}({\mathcal{M}})
,

\begin{split}&\zeta_{t}(\phi)=\frac{1}{t}\int_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}}\phi(r,\varphi)\frac{\cos\varphi d\varphi dr}{2|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|}\quad\text{and}\quad\zeta_{t,N}(\phi)=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{2N}\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\phi(r_{j},\varphi)\cos\varphi d\varphi.\end{split}

Note that
{\mathcal{D}}_{t}(\phi)=\zeta_{t}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}})
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{t,N}(\phi)=\zeta_{t,N}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}})
. Moreover,
\lim_{N\to\infty}\zeta_{t,N}=\zeta_{t}
on continuous functions because
\{r_{j}\}_{j}
is a
|\pi_{r}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})|/N
-dense grid in
\pi_{r}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})
. However, we cannot conclude directly because
\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}
might be discontinuous on
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
and we must argue by approximation. Fix any
\varepsilon>0
. There exists a continuous function
g_{\varepsilon}
such that
g_{\varepsilon}=\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}
on
{\mathcal{M}}\setminus[{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]_{\varepsilon}
and
\|g_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant\|\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant\|\phi\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{0}}
. Moreover,

\zeta_{t}([{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]_{\varepsilon})=\frac{1}{t}\int_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{[{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]_{\varepsilon}}\frac{\cos\varphi d\varphi dr}{2|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|}\leqslant\frac{m([{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]_{\varepsilon}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{t})}{2|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|t}.

As already mentioned,
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
belong to the small stable cone and
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
are horizontal lines. Hence,
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
consists of a finite union of smooth curves transversal to the vertical direction. Therefore,
m([{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]_{\varepsilon}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{t})\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}t\varepsilon
for some
C_{\mathrm{cone}}>0
and
\zeta_{t}([{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]_{\varepsilon})\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}\varepsilon
. Because it is supported on vertical lines, a similar bound holds for
\zeta_{t,N}
, yielding, uniformly in
N
,

\max\{\zeta_{t}([{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]_{\varepsilon}),\zeta_{t,N}([{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]_{\varepsilon})\}\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}\varepsilon.

Therefore,

|\zeta_{t}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}})-\zeta_{t}(g_{\varepsilon})|\leqslant\|\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}-g_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\zeta_{t}([{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]_{\varepsilon})\leqslant 2C_{\mathrm{cone}}\|\phi\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{0}}\varepsilon,

and, uniformly in
N
,

\begin{split}|\zeta_{t,N}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}})-\zeta_{t,N}(g_{\varepsilon})|\leqslant\|\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}-g_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\zeta_{t,N}([{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]_{\varepsilon})\leqslant 2C_{\mathrm{cone}}\|\phi\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{0}}\varepsilon.\end{split}

Let
N
be big enough such that
|\zeta_{t,N}(g_{\varepsilon})-\zeta_{t}(g_{\varepsilon})|\leqslant\varepsilon
. Then, using the last two equations,

\begin{split}|{\mathcal{D}}_{t,N}(\phi)&-{\mathcal{D}}_{t}(\phi)|=|\zeta_{t,N}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}})-\zeta_{t}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}})|\leqslant|\zeta_{t,N}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}})-\zeta_{t,N}(g_{\varepsilon})|\\
&+|\zeta_{t,N}(g_{\varepsilon})-\zeta_{t}(g_{\varepsilon})|+|\zeta_{t}(g_{\varepsilon})-\zeta_{t}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}})|\leqslant 4C_{\mathrm{cone}}\|\phi\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{0}}\varepsilon+\varepsilon.\end{split}

By the arbitrariness of
\varepsilon
this shows that
\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathcal{D}}_{t,N}={\mathcal{D}}_{t}
on continuous functions and
{\mathcal{D}}_{t}
is a initial regular measure. As for
\mu_{0}
, by invariance under
{\mathcal{F}}
,

\mu_{0}(\phi)=\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}})=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}(r,\varphi)\frac{\cos\varphi d\varphi dr}{2|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|}.

We can then approximate the equation above with measures
\mu_{0,N}
supported on denser and denser vertical lines as in (
5.14
) and by transversality one obtains that
\max\{\mu_{0}([{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]_{\varepsilon}),\mu_{0,N}([{\mathcal{S}}_{1}]_{\varepsilon})\}\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}\varepsilon
. Hence, we can repeat the same argument as above and conclude that
\mu_{0}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}
for a sequence of initial regular standard families
\{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}\}_{k}
. This concludes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

Proof of Corollary
4.8
.

Let
t_{0},C>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
be given by Lemma
5.7
. By (
2.6
), we have that
\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{k}\mu_{0}=\mu_{t}
. Hence, for any initial regular standard family
{\mathcal{G}}
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}

(5.15)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:decay-to-mut}|{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}-\mu_{t}|=\lim_{m\to\infty}|{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}-{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{m}\mu_{0})|.

By Lemma
5.8
,
\mu_{0}
is an initial regular measure and, by Lemma
5.6
,
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{m}\mu_{0}
is a regular measure. In other words
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{m}\mu_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\omega_{2},B_{2},D_{2}}
for any
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Since we are free to choose any arbitrary
\varpi_{1},B_{1},D_{1}
as initial conditions in Proposition
4.4
, we may consider measures in
{\mathcal{M}}_{\omega_{2},B_{2},D_{2}}
as initial regular measures. Therefore, possibly with a larger
C>0
, Lemma
5.7
holds for measures in
{\mathcal{M}}_{\omega_{2},B_{2},D_{2}}
, and the expression inside the limit in (
5.15
) is less than
C\gamma^{n}\|\phi\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}
for any
m
. This concludes the proof of the Corollary.
∎

In the next Lemma we establish some sort of independence, uniform in time, between the event
{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}
of surviving up to time
n
in the future and the event
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})
of ending up in the hole one iteration in the past, when both events are considered w.r.t.​ the SRB measure
\mu_{0}
.

Lemma 5.9
.

There exist
C,t_{0}>0
such that, for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

\frac{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}\leqslant Ct.

Proof.

Using that
\mu_{0}
is
{\mathcal{F}}
-invariant,

\begin{split}\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})&=\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})-\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})=\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})-\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n+1})\\
&=\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}\cap{\mathcal{F}}^{-n-1}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})).\end{split}

Hence, recalling the expression (
2.5
) for
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}
,

\frac{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}=\frac{\mu_{0}\bigl(({\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}}\circ{\mathcal{F}})\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}}\bigr)}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}=({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{0})({\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}}\circ{\mathcal{F}}).

By Lemma
5.8
,
\mu_{0}
is a initial regular measure. Hence, for
t_{0}>0
small enough, we can apply Lemma
5.6
, obtaining that
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{0}
is a regular measure for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
. Moreover,
{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}}\circ{\mathcal{F}}
is discontinuous at most on
{\mathcal{S}}_{1,t}
. Therefore, by Lemma
5.4
, there exists a sequence
\{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}\}_{k}
of regular standard families such that

({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{0})({\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}}\circ{\mathcal{F}})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}({\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}}\circ{\mathcal{F}}).

Finally, by Equation (
4.4
), there exists
C>0
such that

|\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}({\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}}\circ{\mathcal{F}})|\leqslant|\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{k}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}))|\leqslant Ct

uniformly in
k
. This concludes the proof.
∎

The next one is the crucial computation involving decay of correlations.

Lemma 5.10
.

There exist
C,t_{0}>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
such that, for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
,
t\in[0,t_{0}]
and
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{1}({\mathcal{M}})
, we have

|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n+1}\mu_{0})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{0})(\phi)|\leqslant Ct\gamma^{n}\|\phi\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}.

Proof.

Partitioning
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t})\cup{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})
and using that
\mu_{0}
is
{\mathcal{F}}
-invariant,

\begin{split}\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}&=\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}({\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t})}+{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})}){\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}\\
&=\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}+\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}\\
&=\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n+1}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n+1}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}+\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}.\end{split}

Therefore, for any
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}({\mathcal{M}})
and
n\geqslant 1
,

\begin{split}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n+1}\mu_{0})&(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{0})(\phi)=\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n+1}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n+1}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n+1})}-\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}\\
&=\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n+1}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n+1}})\biggl(\frac{1}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n+1})}-\frac{1}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}\biggr)-\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}\\
&=\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n+1}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n+1}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n+1})}\biggl(\frac{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})-\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n+1})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}\biggr)\\[10.0pt]
&\hskip 142.26378pt-\frac{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}.\end{split}

Since
\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})-\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n+1})=\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})-\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})=\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})
, and recalling the definition (
5.13
) of
{\mathcal{D}}_{t}
,

(5.16)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:direct-computation}\begin{split}&({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n+1}\mu_{0})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{0})(\phi)\\[10.0pt]
&=\frac{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}\biggl(\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n+1}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n+1}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n+1})}-\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}\biggr)\\[10.0pt]
&=\frac{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}\bigl(({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n+1}\mu_{0})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{D}}_{t})(\phi)\bigr).\end{split}

Therefore, by Lemma
5.9
, there exists
C>0
such that, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

(5.17)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:first-part-decay-auxiliary}\begin{split}|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n+1}\mu_{0})(\phi)&-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{0})(\phi)|\leqslant Ct\bigl|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n+1}\mu_{0})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{D}}_{t})(\phi)\bigr|.\end{split}

Let
t_{0}>0
be small enough. By Lemma
5.8
,
\mu_{0}
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{t}
are initial regular measures and, by Lemma
5.6
,
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}\mu_{0}
is a regular measure for any
t\in[0,t_{0}]
. Therefore, by considering regular measures as initial regular measures (note that Proposition
4.4
holds for any
\varpi_{1}
,
B_{1}
,
D_{1}
), we can apply Lemma
5.7
, possibly with a bigger
C>0
, to the measures
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}\mu_{0}
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{t}
. This implies that, for some
C>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
, we have, for any
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{1}({\mathcal{M}})
,

\bigl|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n+1}\mu_{0})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{D}}_{t})(\phi)\bigr|\leqslant C\gamma^{n}\|\phi\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}.

This, together with (
5.17
), proves the statement of the Lemma for all
n\geqslant 1
. For
n=0
, using the invariance of the measure,

\begin{split}&({\mathcal{L}}_{t}\mu_{0})(\phi)-\mu_{0}(\phi)=\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{1}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{1})}-\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}({\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{1}}+{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})}))\\
&=\mu_{0}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}))\biggl(\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{1}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{1})}-\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}))}\biggr).\end{split}

For
t
small enough the hole and its preimage are disjoint so that
\mu_{0}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}))=2t
and

(5.18)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:nasty-zero-case}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}\mu_{0})(\phi)-\mu_{0}(\phi)=t\biggl(2\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{1}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{1})}-\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}})}{t}-\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}})}{t}\biggr).

Therefore, we obtain the bound
|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}\mu_{0})(\phi)-\mu_{0}(\phi)|\leqslant 4\|\phi\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{0}}t
.
∎

By the previous result, it follows that the modulus of continuity is of order one. Indeed,

(5.19)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:formal-power-series}|\mu_{t}(\phi)-\mu_{0}(\phi)|=\bigl|\lim_{n\to\infty}({\mathcal{L}}^{n}_{t}\mu_{0})(\phi)-\mu_{0}(\phi)\bigr|\leqslant\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\bigl|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{k+1}\mu_{0})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{k}\mu_{0})(\phi)\bigr|,

and by Lemma
5.10
the above is of order
t
for
