\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{1}({\mathcal{M}})
. We now turn to the original problem of Linear response. We start with a very mild control on the dependence of the preimages of the hole on its size.

Lemma 5.11
.

For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exists a function
h_{n}:[0,t_{0}]\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
,
\lim_{t\to 0}h_{n}(t)=0
, such that

{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\subseteq\bigl[{\mathcal{S}}_{n}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}(\partial{\mathcal{H}}_{t})\bigr]_{h_{n}(t)}.

Proof.

Let
x\in{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})
and let
\overline{x}:={\mathcal{F}}^{n}(x)\in{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
. Let
W_{0}
be a stable curve connecting
\overline{x}
to
\partial{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
. Since
W_{0}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
and vectors in the stable cone have bounded slope,
|W_{0}|\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}t
for some
C_{\mathrm{cone}}>0
. We define
W_{k}
,
k\in\{1,...,n\}
, inductively in the following way. Consider the unique connected component
\widetilde{W}_{k}
of
W_{k}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-1}
containing
{\mathcal{F}}^{-k}(\overline{x})
and set
W_{k+1}={\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k})
. Hence,
\widetilde{W}_{0}
has endpoints in
{\mathcal{S}}_{-1}\cup\partial{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
and
W_{1}={\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{0})
has endpoints in
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\partial{\mathcal{H}}_{t})
. In general, arguing by induction, one has that
W_{k}
is a stable curve containing
{\mathcal{F}}^{-k}(\overline{x})={\mathcal{F}}^{n-k}(x)
whose endpoints belong to
{\mathcal{S}}_{k}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-k}(\partial{\mathcal{H}}_{t})
. Moreover, by Lemma
3.1
, there exists
C>0
such that
|W_{k+1}|=|{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\widetilde{W}_{k})|\leqslant|{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(W_{k})|\leqslant C\sqrt{|W_{k}|}
. Hence, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists
C_{n}>0
such that for all
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

|W_{n}|\leqslant C_{n}|W_{0}|^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}\leqslant C_{n}(C_{\mathrm{cone}}t)^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}.

Because
W_{n}
is a continuous curve connecting
x
to
{\mathcal{S}}_{n}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}(\partial{\mathcal{H}}_{t})
,

\inf_{y\in{\mathcal{S}}_{n}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}(\partial{\mathcal{H}}_{t})}|x-y|\leqslant C_{n}(C_{\mathrm{cone}}t)^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}:=h_{n}(t),

and the statement follows by the fact that
x\in{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})
is arbitrary and
\lim_{t\to 0}h_{n}(t)=0
.
∎

Note that we didn’t prove anything about the uniformity of
h_{n}
in
n
. Hence, Lemma
5.11
is useful only for fixed
n
and very small
t
. This is also the case for the following result.

Lemma 5.12
.

For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\mu_{0}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}\cap\cup_{k=1}^{n}{\mathcal{F}}^{-k}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}))=o(t)
as
t\to 0
.

Proof.

For any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
h_{k}
be the function given by Lemma
5.11
, and let
\theta_{n}=\max_{k\in\{1,...,n\}}h_{k}
. One has that
\lim_{t\to 0}\theta_{n}(t)=0
. Moreover, by Lemma
5.11
and using that
\cos\varphi\leqslant 1
,

(5.20)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:estimate-measure-intersections}\begin{split}\mu_{0}\bigl({\mathcal{H}}_{t}\cap\cup_{k=1}^{n}{\mathcal{F}}^{-k}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\bigr)&\leqslant\frac{1}{2|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|}m\bigl({\mathcal{H}}_{t}\cap\cup_{k=1}^{n}\bigl[{\mathcal{S}}_{k}\cup{\mathcal{F}}^{-k}(\partial{\mathcal{H}}_{t})\bigr]_{h_{k}(t)}\bigr)\\
&=\frac{1}{2|\partial{\mathcal{D}}|}m\bigl({\mathcal{H}}_{t}\cap\bigl[{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}\setminus\partial{\mathcal{H}}_{t}\bigr]_{\theta_{n}(t)}\bigr).\end{split}

In the last inequality we used that the sum starts at
k=1
and not
k=0
.
As we mentioned after Lemma
3.2
, the set
{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}\setminus\partial{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
consists of a finite union of smooth stable curves
\{\alpha_{j}\}_{j}
(except for the two horizontal singularity lines
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}=\{\varphi=\pm\pi/2\}
which are not aligned with
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{s}
but nonetheless we group with the
\{\alpha_{j}\}_{j}
). In particular, there is a bound, uniform in
t
, on the slope that any
\alpha_{j}
can have. I.e., the tangent lines to any
\alpha_{j}
are far from being vertical. Hence, there exists
C_{\mathrm{cone}}>0
such that
m\bigl([\alpha_{j}]_{\theta_{n}(t)}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{t}\bigr)\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}t\theta_{n}(t)
for any
j
and
t
. Moreover, by Lemma
3.4
, the cardinality of
\{\alpha_{j}\}_{j}
is also bounded by some
M_{n}>0
uniform in
t
. Therefore,

m\bigl({\mathcal{H}}_{t}\cap\bigl[{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}\setminus\partial{\mathcal{H}}_{t}\bigr]_{\theta_{n}(t)}\bigr)\leqslant\sum_{j}m({\mathcal{H}}_{t}\cap[\alpha_{j}]_{t})\leqslant M_{n}Ct\theta_{n}(t),

which, together with (
5.20
), proves the statement.
∎

To compute the derivative of
\mu_{t}
we fix
n
and consider separately each term of the series (
5.19
) divided by
t
.

Lemma 5.13
.

For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}({\mathcal{M}})
,

\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n+1}\mu_{0})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{0})(\phi)}{t}=\mu_{0}(\phi)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\frac{\pi}{2}}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n+1}(r_{*},\varphi)\cos\varphi d\varphi,

and, for
n=0
,

\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{({\mathcal{L}}_{t}\mu_{0})(\phi)-\mu_{0}(\phi)}{t}=\mu_{0}(\phi)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\frac{\pi}{2}}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\!\!\phi(r_{*},\varphi)\cos\varphi d\varphi+\mu_{0}(\phi)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\frac{\pi}{2}}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\!\!\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}(r_{*},\varphi)\cos\varphi d\varphi.

Proof.

Since
\mu_{0}
is
{\mathcal{F}}
-invariant and using Lemma
5.12
,

(5.21)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:o(t)}\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})^{c})=\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap\cup_{k=0}^{n}{\mathcal{F}}^{-k}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}))=\mu_{0}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}\cap\cup_{k=1}^{n+1}{\mathcal{F}}^{-k}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}))=o(t).

Hence,

\begin{split}\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})&=\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}))-\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})^{c})=t+o(t).\end{split}

Therefore, using also that
\lim_{t\to 0}\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})=\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}})=1
, we have,

(5.22)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:limit-zero}\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}{t\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}=1.

Moreover, by (
5.21
),

\begin{split}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{D}}_{t})(\phi)&=\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}=\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})})-\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})^{c}})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}))-\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})^{c})}\\[9.0pt]
&=\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})})+\|\phi\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{0}}o(t)}{t+o(t)}.\end{split}

Therefore, using again that
\mu_{0}
is
{\mathcal{F}}
-invariant,

(5.23)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:limit-1}\begin{split}\lim_{t\to 0}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{D}}_{t})(\phi)&=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})})}{t}=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n+1}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}})}{t}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\frac{\pi}{2}}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n+1}(r_{*},\varphi)\cos\varphi d\varphi.\end{split}

Note that the expression on the r.h.s.​ of the first line of the equation above is an average of
\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n+1}
on the hole
{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
centered at
r_{*}
. In order to obtain the last equality, we approximate
\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n+1}
with a continuous function on
{\mathcal{M}}\setminus[{\mathcal{S}}_{n+1}]_{\varepsilon}
and use that fact that
\mu_{0}({\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}}\cap[{\mathcal{S}}_{n+1}]_{\varepsilon})\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}t\varepsilon
by transversality.
Finally, by Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem and the invariance of
\mu_{0}
,

(5.24)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:limit-2}\lim_{t\to 0}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n+1}\mu_{0})(\phi)=\lim_{t\to 0}\int\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n+1}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}}\cos\varphi d\varphi dr=\mu_{0}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n+1})=\mu_{0}(\phi),

where in the first equality we used again that
\lim_{t\to 0}\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})=1
. The expression (
5.16
)

\begin{split}&({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n+1}\mu_{0})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{0})(\phi)=\frac{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}{\mu_{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}\bigl(({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n+1}\mu_{0})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{D}}_{t})(\phi)\bigr),\end{split}

together with (
5.22
), (
5.23
) and (
5.24
), proves the statement for
n\geqslant 1
. The case
n=0
follows by (
5.18
) and the same arguments of above.
∎

Finally, Lemma
5.10
guarantees the needed uniformity to exchange the limit with the sum in the expression for the derivative.

Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

By (
2.6
) and by adding and subtracting
({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{k}\mu_{0})(\phi)
, we obtain the telescopic series

(5.25)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:telescopic}\begin{split}\frac{\mu_{t}(\phi)-\mu_{0}(\phi)}{t}&=\frac{\lim_{n\to\infty}({\mathcal{L}}^{n}_{t}\mu_{0})(\phi)-\mu_{0}(\phi)}{t}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{k+1}\mu_{0})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{k}\mu_{0})(\phi)}{t}.\end{split}

By Lemma
5.10
, there exist
C>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
such that, for
t_{0}>0
small enough, uniformly in
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

(5.26)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:exp-conv-derivative}\biggl|\frac{({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{k+1}\mu_{0})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{k}\mu_{0})(\phi)}{t}\biggr|\leqslant C\|\phi\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}\gamma^{k}.

Moreover, by Lemma
5.13
, for each
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, the limit for
t\to 0
of the l.h.s.​ of (
5.26
) exists. Therefore, we can exchange the limit in
t
with the series and, applying Lemma
5.13
for each
k
we obtain,

\begin{split}\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\mu_{t}(\phi)-\mu_{0}(\phi)}{t}&=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{k+1}\mu_{0})(\phi)-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{k}\mu_{0})(\phi)}{t}\\
&=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mu_{0}(\phi)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\frac{\pi}{2}}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{k}(r_{*},\varphi)\cos\varphi d\varphi.\end{split}

This concludes the proof of the Theorem.
∎

6.
Coupling

In this section we prove Proposition
4.4
and Theorem
4.7
. For the reader interested in those results and not in their application to linear response or vice-versa, we remark that this section is completely independent of Section
5
. The coupling techniques are similar to those developed in
[
25
,
12
]
which use ‘finite time’ holonomies and avoid Cantor rectangles. This is somehow convenient because the dependence on the perturbation might be easier to track in finite time arguments. Note also that the presence of a hole is a novelty in coupling and it requires additional arguments in order to implement the coupling algorithm. Fix any
\varpi_{1},B_{1},D_{1}>0
.

6.1.
Evolution of unstable curves and densities

In this section we consider a single standard pair and we study how the regularity of the unstable curve
W
and density
\rho
are affected by the dynamics.

We start with the invariance for the support of standard pairs, i.e., of the set
\mathcal{W}(D)
of unstable curves introduced in (
4.1
). We do not present a full proof of the required statement since it is well known in the literature, but not immediate. In particular, the fact that the second derivative of unstable curves remains bounded under iterations of the billiard map is not obvious given the blow-up of the derivatives of
{\mathcal{F}}
near singularities. Therefore, we rely on the so-called
curvature bounds
. See below for references to the relevant statement.

Lemma 6.1
.

There exists
D_{2}>0
such that, for any
W\in\mathcal{W}(D_{1})
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists a finite set
\{W_{i}\}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{W}(D_{2})
such that
{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(W)=\cup_{i}W_{i}
.

Proof.

We construct
\{W_{i}\}_{i}
in the following way. Take the connected components
\{\widetilde{W}_{i}\}_{i}
of
W\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
, which are a finite number, and set
W_{i}={\mathcal{F}}^{n}(\widetilde{W}_{i})
. By the requirement on the tangent space of
W
and cone invariance, each tangent line at
W_{i}
is aligned with the small unstable cone field and so
W_{i}
is the graph of a function
\varphi_{W_{i}}
. By differentiability of
{\mathcal{F}}^{n}
on
{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
and the fact that
W
is the graph of a twice differentiable function
\varphi_{W}
each
\varphi_{W_{i}}
is twice differentiable. The fact that
|\varphi_{W_{i}}^{\prime\prime}|\leqslant D_{2}
for
D_{2}>0
big enough follows from
[
16
, Lemma 4.1]
and uses the
{\mathcal{C}}^{3}
regularity of the boundary. Finally, we can ensure that
|W_{i}|\leqslant\delta_{*}
by partitioning each
W_{i}
into several pieces if needed.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

We fix
D_{2}
to be the value given by Lemma
6.1
. In particular, we will always assume that initial standard pairs are supported on unstable curves in
\mathcal{W}(D_{1})
so that their push-forwards will be supported on elements of
\mathcal{W}(D_{2})
. Therefore, when this does not create confusion, we will suppress the dependence on
D
in
\mathcal{W}
.

The next task is to study the evolution of the density. Let
W\in\mathcal{W}
and assume that
W\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{1}=\emptyset
(if not, we consider connected subsets of
W\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
). Recall that
I_{W}=\pi_{r}(W)
and
(r_{1},\varphi_{1})={\mathcal{F}}(r,\varphi)
. We introduce the function
r_{1,W}:I_{W}\to I_{{\mathcal{F}}(W)}
defined by

(6.1)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:def-map-r-coordinate}r_{1,W}(r)=r_{1}(r,\varphi_{W}(r)).

Let
W
be a short unstable curve such that
{\mathcal{F}}^{n}
is smooth on
W
. We define
r_{1,W}^{n}:I_{W}\to I_{{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(W)}
as the composition

(6.2)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:powers-expanding-base}r_{1,W}^{n}(r)=r_{1,{\mathcal{F}}^{n-1}(W)}\circ r_{1,{\mathcal{F}}^{n-2}(W)}\circ...\circ r_{1,W}(r).

Moreover, whenever
r_{1,{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}(W)}^{n}:I_{{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}(W)}\to I_{W}
is well defined, we denote by
r_{1,W}^{-n}:I_{W}\to I_{{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}(W)}
its inverse. These compositions behave essentially as expanding maps, even if a single iteration can be even contracting. We now prove some basic properties of these compositions.
To compare the next statements with other literature, note that
r_{1,W}^{\prime}\sim{\mathcal{J}}_{W}{\mathcal{F}}
, the Jacobian of the map restricted to the unstable curve
W
w.r.t.​ the Euclidean metric. This happens because the small unstable cone has bounded slope. In particular the constant depends only on the cone. In view of this fact, the results in the next three Lemmata are well known, but we include the proofs because of the slightly different conventions. Considering the derivative of
r_{1,W}
in place of
{\mathcal{J}}_{W}{\mathcal{F}}
is natural for our coupling arguments, as it will be clear in the sequel.

Lemma 6.3
.

There exists
c>0
such that, for every
r\in I_{W}
,

|r_{1,W}^{\prime}(r)|\geqslant\frac{c}{\cos\varphi_{1}(r,\varphi_{W}(r))}.

Moreover, there exists
\Lambda>1
such that, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, one has
|{r_{1,W}^{n}}^{\prime}|\geqslant c\Lambda^{n}
.

Proof.

By (
6.1
) and (
3.4
), we have

(6.3)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:derivative-FG}r_{1,W}^{\prime}(r)=\frac{\partial r_{1}}{\partial r}(r,\varphi_{W}(r))+\frac{\partial r_{1}}{\partial\varphi}(r,\varphi_{W}(r))\varphi_{W}^{\prime}(r)=-\frac{\tau\kappa+\cos\varphi_{W}+\tau\varphi_{W}^{\prime}}{\cos\varphi_{1}},

where all the functions in the last expression are evaluated at
(r,\varphi_{W}(r))
. We have that
\cos\varphi_{W}
is positive and, by (
2.1
),
\tau
and
\kappa
are strictly positive. Moreover, because
W
is an unstable curve,
\varphi_{W}^{\prime}
is positive as well. This proves the first statement. As for the second statement, denoting by
W_{k}
the connected components of
{\mathcal{F}}^{k}(W)
containing
(r_{k},\varphi_{k})=x_{k}={\mathcal{F}}^{k}(r,\varphi_{W}(r))
and by
\tau_{k}=\tau(x_{k})
,
\kappa_{k}=\kappa(x_{k})
, we have

\begin{split}{r_{1,W}^{n}}^{\prime}&=\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}-\frac{\tau_{k}\kappa_{k}+\cos\varphi_{k}+\tau_{k}\varphi_{W_{k}}^{\prime}}{\cos\varphi_{k+1}}\\
&=-\frac{\tau_{0}\kappa_{0}+\cos\varphi_{W}+\tau\varphi_{W}^{\prime}}{\cos\varphi_{n}}\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}-\frac{\tau_{k}\kappa_{k}+\cos\varphi_{k}+\tau\varphi_{W_{k}}^{\prime}}{\cos\varphi_{k}}.\end{split}

Here
\varphi_{W_{k}}^{\prime}
is the derivative of the graph of
W_{k}
w.r.t.​ its arc-length parameter
r_{k}
. By cone invariance
\varphi_{W_{k}}^{\prime}\geqslant 0
for all
k
. Hence, we obtain the lower bound

|{r_{1,W}^{n}}^{\prime}|\geqslant\tau_{0}\kappa_{0}\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}(1+\tau_{k}\kappa_{k})\geqslant\frac{\tau_{\mathrm{min}}\kappa_{\mathrm{min}}}{1+\tau_{\mathrm{max}}\kappa_{\mathrm{max}}}(1+\tau_{\mathrm{min}}\kappa_{\mathrm{min}})^{n}.

This concludes the proof of the Lemma with
\Lambda=1+\tau_{\mathrm{min}}\kappa_{\mathrm{min}}>1
.
∎

The next step is studying the distortion.

Lemma 6.4
.

There exists
C>0
such that, for every
r\in I_{W}
,

\biggl|\frac{r_{1,W}^{\prime\prime}}{{r_{1,W}^{\prime}}^{2}}(r)\biggr|\leqslant\frac{C}{\cos\varphi_{1}(r,\varphi_{W}(r))}.

Proof.

Set
\mathbb{g}(r)=(r,\varphi_{W}(r))
. Differentiating (
6.3
) and recalling the expression (
3.4
) for the differential of
{\mathcal{F}}
, we find

(6.4)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:second-deriv-rw}\begin{split}r_{1,W}^{\prime\prime}(r)&=\frac{\bigl(A\circ\mathbb{g}(r)+B\circ\mathbb{g}(r)\varphi_{W}^{\prime}(r)\bigr)}{(\cos\varphi_{1}\circ\mathbb{g}(r))^{2}}(\cos\varphi_{1}\circ\mathbb{g})^{\prime}(r)\\
&-\frac{\bigl((A\circ\mathbb{g})^{\prime}(r)+(B\circ\mathbb{g})^{\prime}(r)\varphi_{W}^{\prime}(r)+B\circ\mathbb{g}(r)\varphi_{W}^{\prime\prime}(r)\bigr)}{\cos\varphi_{1}\circ\mathbb{g}(r)}.\end{split}

We also have

(6.5)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:deriv-cos-image}\begin{split}(\cos\varphi_{1}\circ\mathbb{g})^{\prime}(r)&=-(\varphi_{1}\circ\mathbb{g})^{\prime}(r)\sin\varphi_{1}\circ\mathbb{g}(r)\\
&=\frac{\sin\varphi_{1}}{\cos\varphi_{1}}\circ\mathbb{g}(r)\bigl(C\circ\mathbb{g}(r)+D\circ\mathbb{g}(r)\varphi_{W}^{\prime}(r)\bigr).\end{split}

By (
6.4
) and (
6.5
) and using that both
|\varphi_{W}^{\prime}|
and
|\varphi_{W}^{\prime\prime}|
are bounded and
(A\circ\mathbb{g})^{\prime}
and
(B\circ\mathbb{g})^{\prime}
are bounded by
\mathrm{const}\times(\tau\circ\mathbb{g})^{\prime}\sim 1/(\cos\varphi_{1}\circ\mathbb{g})
, we obtain that
|r_{1,W}^{\prime\prime}(r)|\leqslant\mathrm{const}\times(\cos\varphi_{1}\circ\mathbb{g}(r))^{-3}
. This, together with the first part of Lemma
6.3
, proves the statement.
∎

Lemma
6.4
is discouraging since it hints at the fact that distortion is unbounded near grazing collisions. Homogeneity strips are a tool to control distortion.

Definition 6.5
.

We call
W\in\mathcal{W}
weakly homogeneous if
W\subset{\mathbb{H}}_{k}
for some
k
. We call
W

n
-homogeneous if
{\mathcal{F}}^{-k}(W)
is weakly homogeneous for
k\in\{0,...,n-1\}
.

In practice, homogeneous curves do not ‘feel’ the unbounded distortion because they are small enough compared to their distance to the singularities. The following Lemma is essentially an adaptation of
[
21
, Lemma 5.27]
to our notation.

Lemma 6.6
.

There exists
C>0
such that, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and every
n
-homogeneous
W\in\mathcal{W}
, for all
x,y\in I_{W}
,

\biggl|\ln\frac{{r_{1,W}^{-n}}^{\prime}(x)}{{r_{1,W}^{-n}}^{\prime}(y)}\biggr|\leqslant C|x-y|^{\frac{1}{3}}.

Proof.

Setting
W_{j}={\mathcal{F}}^{-j}(W)
and by the chain rule,

\begin{split}\ln\frac{{r_{1,W}^{-n}}^{\prime}(x)}{{r_{1,W}^{-n}}^{\prime}(y)}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\ln r_{1,W_{j}}^{\prime}\circ r_{1,W}^{-j}(y)-\ln r_{1,W_{j}}^{\prime}\circ r_{1,W}^{-j}(x).\end{split}

Moreover,

\begin{split}|\ln r_{1,W_{j}}^{\prime}\circ&r_{1,W}^{-j}(y)-\ln r^{\prime}_{1,W_{j}}\circ r_{1,W}^{-j}(x)|\\
&=|\ln r_{1,W_{j}}^{\prime}\circ r_{1,W_{j-1}}^{-1}(r_{1,W}^{-j+1}(y))-\ln r_{1,W_{j}}^{\prime}\circ r_{1,W_{j-1}}^{-1}(r_{1,W}^{-j+1}(x))|.\end{split}

By the intermediate value theorem, the quantity above is less than

|r_{1,W}^{-j+1}(y)-r_{1,W}^{-j+1}(x)|\max_{I_{W_{j}}}\biggl|\frac{r_{1,W_{j}}^{\prime\prime}}{(r_{1,W_{j}}^{\prime})^{2}}\biggr|.

Using that
|r_{1,W}^{-j+1}(y)-r_{1,W}^{-j+1}(x)|\leqslant|I_{W_{j-1}}|\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}|{\mathcal{F}}^{-j+1}(W)|
and Lemma
6.4
, we can further bound the expression above with

C_{\mathrm{cone}}|r_{1,W}^{-j+1}(y)-r_{1,W}^{-j+1}(x)|^{\frac{1}{3}}\frac{|{\mathcal{F}}^{-j+1}(W)|^{\frac{2}{3}}}{\min_{\varphi\in\pi_{\varphi}({\mathcal{F}}^{-j+1}(W))}\cos\varphi}\leqslant C|r_{1,W}^{-j+1}(y)-r_{1,W}^{-j+1}(x)|^{\frac{1}{3}},

for some
C>0
. In the inequality we used Lemma
3.2
together with the fact that
{\mathcal{F}}^{-j+1}(W)
is weakly-homogeneous. Finally, by the second part of Lemma
6.3
, for some
\Lambda>1
,

\begin{split}\biggl|\ln\frac{{r_{1,W}^{-n}}^{\prime}(x)}{{r_{1,W}^{-n}}^{\prime}(y)}\biggr|&\leqslant C\sum_{j=1}^{n}|r_{1,W}^{-j+1}(y)-r_{1,W}^{-j+1}(x)|^{\frac{1}{3}}\leqslant C\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Lambda^{\frac{-j+1}{3}}|x-y|^{\frac{1}{3}}\leqslant C|x-y|^{\frac{1}{3}}.\end{split}

This concludes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

We now consider the evolution of densities supported on unstable curves and prove that densities of some regularity are preserved by the dynamics. We also observe that densities with arbitrarily bad regularity recover exponentially fast. For any
n
-homogeneous unstable curve
W
and
\rho:I_{{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}(W)}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
, we let
{\mathcal{L}}_{W}^{n}\rho:I_{W}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
be

(6.6)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:transfer-op}{\mathcal{L}}_{W}^{n}\rho=\rho\circ r_{1,W}^{-n}{r_{1,W}^{-n}}^{\prime}.

This defines a rule of evolution for densities supported on unstable curves. We may drop
W
from the notation and write simply
{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho
when this does not create confusion. Recall
{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi)
from (
4.2
). In the following Lemma we prove that
{\mathcal{L}}
leaves invariant the cone
{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi)
with
\varpi
big enough.

Lemma 6.7
.

There exist
Q_{1},Q_{2}>0
,
\Lambda_{1}>3
and
\overline{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for any
n\geqslant\overline{n}
,
\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi)
:

a)

If
W
is
1
-homogeneous and
\rho:I_{{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(W)}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
,
{\mathcal{L}}\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}(Q_{2}\varpi+Q_{1})
;

b)

If
W
is
n
-homogeneous and
\rho:I_{{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}(W)}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
,
{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}(\Lambda_{1}^{-1}\varpi+Q_{1})
.

Proof.

Recalling (
6.6
) we obtain, for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
x,y\in I_{W}
,

\frac{{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho(x)}{{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho(y)}=\frac{\rho\circ r_{1,W}^{-n}(x)}{\rho\circ r_{1,W}^{-n}(y)}\frac{{r_{1,W}^{-n}}^{\prime}(x)}{{r_{1,W}^{-n}}^{\prime}(y)}.

Let
\overline{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}
be such that, according to Lemma
6.3
, it holds
|{r_{1,W}^{-\overline{n}}}^{\prime}|\leqslant C\Lambda^{-\overline{n}}\leqslant\Lambda_{1}^{-1}<1/3
. Let
W
be
n
-homogeneous and pick
x,y\in I_{W}
. By Lemma
6.6
and the equation above, and using that
\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi)
, for
Q_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
big enough and for any
n\geqslant\overline{n}
,

(6.7)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:invariance-densities-final}\begin{split}\biggl|\ln\frac{{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho(x)}{{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho(y)}\biggr|&\leqslant\varpi|r_{1,W}^{-n}(x)-r_{1,W}^{-n}(y)|^{\frac{1}{3}}+Q_{1}|x-y|^{\frac{1}{3}}\\
&\leqslant(\Lambda_{1}^{-1}\varpi+Q_{1})|x-y|^{\frac{1}{3}}.\end{split}

This concludes the proof of the second part of the Lemma. As for the first part, by the first line of (
6.7
) in the case
n=1
, we have,

\begin{split}\biggl|\ln\frac{{\mathcal{L}}\rho(x)}{{\mathcal{L}}\rho(y)}\biggr|&\leqslant\varpi|r_{1,W}^{-1}(x)-r_{1,W}^{-1}(y)|^{\frac{1}{3}}+Q_{1}|x-y|^{\frac{1}{3}}\\
&\leqslant(Q_{2}\varpi+Q_{1})|x-y|^{\frac{1}{3}},\end{split}

where we used the lower bound
|r_{1,W}^{\prime}|>c
in Lemma
6.3
. This concludes the proof.
∎

In order to have good distortion properties, we will always consider the functions
{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho
restricted to
n
-homogeneous unstable curves. This is a consequence of how we define the evolution of standard families in Section
6.2
, treating
\mathbb{S}
as real discontinuities. Let
\Lambda_{1}
,
Q_{1},Q_{2}
and
\overline{n}
be given by Lemma
6.7
. Set

(6.8)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:contstant-omega2-regularity}\varpi_{2}=100(Q_{2}+Q_{1}+1)^{\overline{n}}\max\biggl\{\frac{Q_{1}}{1-\Lambda_{1}^{-1}},\varpi_{1}\biggr\}.

As a Corollary of Lemma
6.7
, we obtain invariance of regular densities. In the following statement, whose proof follows by a direct computation, we let
W
be an
n
-homogeneous unstable curve and
\rho
be a density on
I_{{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}(W)}
.

Corollary 6.8
.

There exists
\Omega>1
such that:

a)

For any
\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi_{1})
,
{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi_{2})
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
;

b)

For any
\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi)
with
\varpi>\varpi_{2}/2
, then
{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi_{2}/2)
for all
n\geqslant\overline{n}
;

c)

For any
\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi)
,
{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}(\max\{\Omega\varpi,\varpi_{2}\})
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

As a byproduct of our coupling argument we will be forced to consider densities that lie in
{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi)
with
\varpi
arbitrarily big. Thanks to part b) of Corollary
6.8
densities recover exponentially fast. However, in this transient, the estimates for the evolution of
\mathcal{Z}
would be out of control if we were to rely only on the cones
{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi)
. To overcome this problem, we will consider yet another type of cone given by the ratio
\max\rho/\min\rho
. This cone has the advantage on the one hand to bound the oscillations of the density
\rho
and on the other hand to be ‘less demanding’ than
{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi)
so that the bad densities that we will consider have a max/min ratio of order one. We cannot rely on those cones only because there is no recovery. However, they have the important property of being ‘almost preserved’ by the dynamics.

Lemma 6.9
.

There exists
Q_{3}>0
such that, for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
W\in\mathcal{W}

n
-homogeneous and
\rho:I_{{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}(W)}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
,

\frac{\max_{I_{W}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho}{\min_{I_{W}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho}\leqslant Q_{3}\frac{\max_{I_{{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}(W)}}\rho}{\min_{{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}(W)}\rho}.

Proof.

We have

\frac{\max_{I_{W}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho}{\min_{I_{W}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho}=\frac{\max_{I_{W}}\rho\circ r_{1,W}^{-n}{r_{1,W}^{-n}}^{\prime}}{\min_{I_{W}}\rho\circ r_{1,W}^{-n}{r_{1,W}^{-n}}^{\prime}}\leqslant\frac{\max_{I_{{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}(W)}}\rho}{\min_{I_{{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}(W)}}\rho}\frac{\max_{I_{W}}{r_{1,W}^{-n}}^{\prime}}{\min_{I_{W}}{r_{1,W}^{-n}}^{\prime}}.

By Lemma
6.6
, there exists
C>0
such that the ratio between the derivatives in the r.h.s.​ is less than
e^{C|I_{W}|^{1/3}}\leqslant e^{C|W|^{1/3}}\leqslant e^{C\delta_{*}^{1/3}}\leqslant e^{C}:=Q_{3}
. This concludes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

We conclude this section with a classic for cones.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi)
,
\varpi>0
, defined on
I
. Then
\min\left\{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\right\}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi)
and, if
\rho_{1}>\rho_{2}
, then

\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}}\biggl(\varpi\biggl(1+2\sup_{I}(\rho_{1}/\rho_{2}-1)^{-1}\biggr)\biggr).

Proof.

For
x,y\in I
,

\begin{split}\frac{\min\left\{\rho_{1}(x),\rho_{2}(x)\right\}}{\min\left\{\rho_{1}(y),\rho_{2}(y)\right\}}\leqslant\frac{\min\{\rho_{1}(y)e^{\varpi|x-y|^{\frac{1}{3}}},\rho_{2}(y)e^{\varpi|x-y|^{\frac{1}{3}}}\}}{\min\left\{\rho_{1}(y),\rho_{2}(y)\right\}}\leqslant e^{\varpi|x-y|^{\frac{1}{3}}},\end{split}

proving the first part. And,

\begin{split}\frac{\rho_{1}(x)-\rho_{2}(x)}{\rho_{1}(y)-\rho_{2}(y)}&\leqslant\frac{e^{\varpi|x-y|^{\frac{1}{3}}}\left(\rho_{1}(y)-e^{-2\varpi|x-y|^{\frac{1}{3}}}\rho_{2}(y)\right)}{\rho_{1}(y)-\rho_{2}(y)}\\
&\leqslant e^{\varpi|x-y|^{\frac{1}{3}}}\left(1+2\varpi|x-y|^{\frac{1}{3}}\frac{\rho_{2}(y)}{\rho_{1}(y)-\rho_{2}(y)}\right)\\
&\leqslant e^{\varpi|x-y|^{\frac{1}{3}}\left(1+2\sup_{I}\frac{\rho_{2}}{\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}}\right)}.\end{split}

This concludes the proof.
∎

6.2.
Standard families and their evolution

In this section we define the evolution of standard families.

We find it convenient to define the action of
{\mathcal{F}}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
on standard families in a canonical way. We consider three possible evolutions for a standard family. The first is induced by the standard billiard map
{\mathcal{F}}
. The second consists of evolving via the map
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}_{t}
which acts as
{\mathcal{F}}
but has the additional discontinuities
\Xi_{n,t}
due to the boundary of the hole. The third evolution
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
is given by the leaky system and we renormalize by the surviving mass. We slightly abuse the notation by using the same symbol
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
for both the evolution of measures (
2.5
) and of standard families. To make this precise, we first consider a single standard pair
\ell=(W,\rho)
. Let
\{W_{j}\}
be the connected components of
W\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{n}^{{\mathbb{H}}}
,
\{\widehat{W}_{j}\}
the connected components of
W\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}^{{\mathbb{H}}}
and
\{V_{j}\}
the connected components of
(W\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}^{{\mathbb{H}}})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}
. Note that the
n^{th}
images of all these curves are
n
-homogeneous. Set

(6.9)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:density-weights-evolution}\begin{split}&\rho_{A,j}=\frac{{\mathcal{L}}^{n}(\rho{\mathbbm{1}}_{I_{W_{j}}})}{\int_{I_{W_{j}}}\rho},\quad p_{A,j}=\int_{I_{W_{j}}}\rho,\quad\rho_{B,j}=\frac{{\mathcal{L}}^{n}(\rho{\mathbbm{1}}_{I_{\widehat{W}_{j}}})}{\int_{I_{\widehat{W}_{j}}}\rho},\quad p_{B,j}=\int_{I_{\widehat{W}_{j}}}\rho,\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\rho_{C,j}=\frac{{\mathcal{L}}^{n}(\rho{\mathbbm{1}}_{I_{V_{j}}})}{\int_{I_{V_{j}}}\rho},\quad p_{C,j}=\frac{\int_{I_{V_{j}}}\rho}{\mu_{\ell}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}.\end{split}

These are the densities and weights of the evolved standard pairs. More precisely, we set
{\mathcal{F}}^{n}\ell=\{(p_{A,j},{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(W_{j}),\rho_{A,j})\}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}\ell=\{(p_{B,j},{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(\widehat{W}_{j}),\rho_{B,j})\}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\ell=\{(p_{C,j},{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(V_{j}),\rho_{C,j})\}
.
Note the total remaining mass in the denominator for the weights of
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\ell
, so that
\mu_{{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\ell}
is a probability measure. It is possible that
|{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(W_{j})|\geqslant\delta_{*}
for some
W_{j}
(or
\widehat{W}_{j}
,
V_{j}
). In this case, we partition
{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(W_{j})
into at most
\lfloor|{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(W_{j})|/\delta_{*}\rfloor+1
pieces which become the defining unstable curves in
{\mathcal{F}}^{n}\ell
. In this case, the weights
p_{A,j}
are the integral of
\rho
over the
n^{\text{th}}
-preimage of the new pieces. Finally, given a standard family
{\mathcal{G}}=\{(p_{k},W_{k},\rho_{k})\}
, we set
{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathcal{G}}=\{(p_{k}p_{A,j,k},{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(W_{k,j}),\rho_{A,k,j})\}
where
p_{A,j,k}
,
W_{k,j}
and
\rho_{A,k,j}
are defined as above, only relatively to the standard pair
\ell_{k}=(W_{k},\rho_{k})
in place of
\ell=(W,\rho)
. The standard families
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}}
are defined analogously with the only caveat that
p_{C,j,k}
need to be divided by the surviving mass
\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})
and not just the mass lost by the standard pair
\ell_{k}=(V_{k},\rho_{k})
. (This happens because
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
is not a linear operator). The next Lemma clarifies the raison d’être of these definitions.

Lemma 6.11
.

For any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and standard family
{\mathcal{G}}
,
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}({\mathcal{M}})
and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}})(\phi)=\mu_{{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}}}(\phi)\quad\text{and}\quad\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n})=\mu_{{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathcal{G}}}(\phi)=\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}}}(\phi).

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=\{(p_{k},\ell_{k})\}
and
\ell_{k}=(W_{k},\rho_{k})
. Recalling that
W_{k}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}=\cup_{j}V_{k,j}
,

\begin{split}\mu_{\ell_{k}}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})=\sum_{j}\int_{I_{V_{k,j}}}\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(r,\varphi_{V_{k,j}}(r))\rho_{k}(r)dr.\end{split}

Doing the change of variable
r\to r^{\prime}
specified by
{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(r,\varphi_{V_{k,j}}(r))=(r^{\prime},\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(V_{k,j})}(r^{\prime}))
, we obtain

(6.10)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:single-sp-push-forward}\begin{split}\mu_{\ell_{k}}(\phi\circ&{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})=\sum_{j}\int_{I_{{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(V_{k,j})}}\phi(r^{\prime},\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(V_{k,j})}(r^{\prime})){\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho_{k}(r^{\prime})dr^{\prime}\\
&=\sum_{j}\int_{I_{V_{k,j}}}\rho_{k}\int_{I_{{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(V_{k,j})}}\phi(r^{\prime},\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(V_{k,j})}(r^{\prime}))\frac{{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho_{k}(r^{\prime})dr^{\prime}}{\int_{I_{{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(V_{k,j})}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho_{k}},\end{split}

where in the equality we divided and multiplied by
\int_{I_{V_{k,j}}}\rho_{k}=\int_{I_{{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(V_{k,j})}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho_{k}
. By (
6.10
), and recalling (
6.9
) and the definitions given afterward,

({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}})(\phi)=\frac{\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})}{\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}=\frac{\sum_{k}p_{k}\mu_{\ell_{k}}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})}{\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}=\mu_{{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}}}(\phi).

This proves the first part of the statement. The fact that
\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}(\phi\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n})=\mu_{{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathcal{G}}}(\phi)
follows from the first equation in the statement in the case
t=0
and the fact that
\mu_{{\mathcal{F}}^{n}{\mathcal{G}}}(\phi)=\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}}}(\phi)
follows from the fact that the maps
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
and
{\mathcal{F}}
act in the same way at the level of measures and standard families.
∎

6.3.
Invariance

In this section we establish a Growth Lemma: this is an important tool that guarantees that expansion of unstable curves beats their fragmentation. We prove a Growth Lemma for
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
and a weaker version for
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
. An important difference between the two statements is that, for the dynamics with the hole, we can show invariance but we cannot guarantee that arbitrarily small unstable curves become regular families after enough iterations. The physical interpretation is clear: a small piece could just end up in the hole before recovering. We use these results to prove Proposition
4.4
about invariance of standard families.

Recall the definition of the boundary
\mathcal{Z}
from equation (
4.3
). The following simple Lemma clarifies in which sense
\mathcal{Z}
determines the proportion of long unstable curves in a family.

Lemma 6.12
.

For any
\zeta>0
and standard family
{\mathcal{G}}=\{(p_{j},\ell_{j})\}
, we have

\sum_{j:|W_{j}|\leqslant\zeta}p_{j}\leqslant\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}})\zeta.

Proof.

We have

\sum_{j:|W_{j}|\leqslant\zeta}p_{j}\leqslant\sum_{j:|W_{j}|\leqslant\zeta}p_{j}\frac{\zeta}{|W_{j}|}=\zeta\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}}).

∎

We now introduce a result from
[
30
]
. For an unstable vector
(dr,d\varphi)\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{u}
, set

(6.11)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:adapted-norm}\|(dr,d\varphi)\|_{*}=\biggl(\kappa+\biggl|\frac{d\varphi}{dr}\biggr|\biggr)\frac{\sqrt{dr^{2}+d\varphi^{2}}}{\sqrt{1+\biggl(\frac{d\varphi}{dr}\biggr)^{2}}}=\biggl(\kappa+\biggl|\frac{d\varphi}{dr}\biggr|\biggr)|dr|.

By
[
21
, Exercise 5.55]
,
\|\cdot\|_{*}
is an adapted norm for the billiard map. Moreover, since
C_{\mathrm{cone}}^{-1}\leqslant|d\varphi/dr|\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}
for vectors in the unstable cone, the metric (
6.11
) is equivalent to the Euclidean metric in measuring the length of unstable curves. Recall the notation
\widehat{W}_{j}
for the connected components of
W\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}^{{\mathbb{H}}}
of an unstable curve
W
and denote by
\widehat{W}^{n}_{j}={\mathcal{F}}^{n}(\widehat{W}_{j})={\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}(\widehat{W}_{j})
. Let also
|J_{\widehat{W}_{j}}{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}|_{*}
denote the smallest contraction on
\widehat{W}^{n}_{j}
under
{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}
in the metric induced by the adapted norm (
6.11
).

Lemma 6.13

(
[
30
, Lemma 8.4]
)
.

There exists
\theta_{*}<1
,
B_{0}>0
and a sequence
\delta_{n}\searrow 0
such that

\sup_{W\in\mathcal{W},\text{ }|W|\leqslant\delta_{n}}\sum_{j}|J_{\widehat{W}^{n}_{j}}{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}|_{*}\leqslant(1+n(B_{0}-1))\theta_{*}^{n}.

Here, the result is stated for powers
{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}
of the inverse map and unstable curves, while the original statement in
[
30
]
has
{\mathcal{F}}^{n}
in place of
{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}
and stable curves in place of unstable curves. The two statements are equivalent due to the time reversibility of the billiard. Moreover
\theta_{*}
is characterized as the one-step contraction of
\mathcal{Z}
and
B_{0}
as the number of pieces in which an unstable (stable in the original reference) curve can be cut by
\partial{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
. In our case
B_{0}=3
but we won’t need to be that precise. Fix some
\gamma\in(0,1)
.

Lemma 6.14

(Growth Lemma)
.

There exist
n_{*}\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
Z_{0}>0
such that the following is true. Let
{\mathcal{G}}=\{(p_{k},W_{k},\rho_{k})\}
be a standard family such that, for every
k
,
\max\rho_{k}/\min\rho_{k}\leqslant 2e^{\varpi_{2}}
. Then, for all
t\in[0,t_{0}]
and
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{(p+1)n_{*}}_{t}{\mathcal{G}})\leqslant\gamma\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{pn_{*}}_{t}{\mathcal{G}})+Z_{0}.

Moreover, there exists
Z_{1}>1
such that, for all
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{p+1}{\mathcal{G}})\leqslant Z_{1}\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{p}{\mathcal{G}}).

Proof.

Because of the assumption on the max-min ratio of the initial densities and by Lemma
6.9
, the standard families
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{p}{\mathcal{G}}
support densities
\rho^{\prime}_{j}
with
\max\rho^{\prime}_{j}/\min\rho^{\prime}_{j}\leqslant 2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}
for all
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
. (Recall that by definition the unstable curves in
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{p}{\mathcal{G}}
are
p
-homogeneous). Hence, without loss of generality, we prove the Lemma in the case
p=0
assuming that

\max\rho_{k}/\min\rho_{k}\leqslant 2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}

for all
k
. For any
W_{k}
denote by
\{\widehat{W}_{k,j}\}
the connected components of
W_{k}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}^{{\mathbb{H}}}
and by
\widehat{W}_{k,j}^{n}={\mathcal{F}}^{n}(\widehat{W}_{k,j})
. Call
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
the corresponding ‘extended’ standard family composed of the curves
\{\widehat{W}_{k,j}^{n}\}
.
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
is not a standard family as its unstable curves may be longer than
\delta_{*}
. We will partition each curve in
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
shortly and estimate the effect on the boundary. For the moment, we observe that

(6.12)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:first-tildeG}\mathcal{Z}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n})=\sum_{k}p_{k}\sum_{j}\int_{I_{\widehat{W}_{k,j}}}\rho_{k}\frac{1}{|\widehat{W}_{k,j}^{n}|}\leqslant C_{\mathrm{metric}}\sum_{k}p_{k}\sum_{j}\int_{I_{\widehat{W}_{k,j}}}\rho_{k}\frac{|J_{\widehat{W}^{n}_{k,j}}{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}|_{*}}{|\widehat{W}_{k,j}|},

where the constant
C_{\mathrm{metric}}>0
is given by the equivalence between the Euclidean metric
|\cdot|
and the adapted metric
|\cdot|_{*}
on vectors in the small unstable cone. Moreover, using that
\rho_{k}
are probability densities on
I_{W_{k}}
and
\max\rho_{k}/\min\rho_{k}\leqslant 2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}
, we have,

(6.13)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:unifor-density-effect}\int_{I_{\widehat{W}_{k,j}}}\rho_{k}\leqslant 2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}\frac{|I_{\widehat{W}_{k,j}}|}{|I_{W_{k}}|}.

Therefore, since
|I_{\widehat{W}_{k,j}}|\leqslant|\widehat{W}_{k,j}|
and
|W_{k}|\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}|I_{W_{k}}|
,

(6.14)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:intermidiate-ZtildeG}\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n})&\leqslant C_{\mathrm{metric}}2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}\sum_{k}p_{k}\sum_{j}\frac{|I_{\widehat{W}_{k,j}}|}{|I_{W_{k}}|}\frac{|J_{\widehat{W}^{n}_{k,j}}{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}|_{*}}{|\widehat{W}_{k,j}|}\\
&\leqslant C_{\mathrm{metric}}2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}\sum_{k}p_{k}\frac{1}{|I_{W_{k}}|}\sum_{j}|J_{\widehat{W}^{n}_{k,j}}{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}|_{*}\\
&\leqslant 2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}C_{\mathrm{cone}}C_{\mathrm{metric}}\sum_{k}p_{k}\frac{1}{|W_{k}|}\sum_{j}|J_{\widehat{W}^{n}_{k,j}}{\mathcal{F}}^{-n}|_{*}.\end{split}

Recall that
\gamma\in(0,1)
is the sought contraction. Let
n_{*}\in{\mathbb{N}}
be the smallest natural number such that, according to Lemma
6.13
, for all
|W_{k}|\leqslant\delta_{n_{*}}
, we have

(6.15)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:tildegamma-contraction}\begin{split}2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}C_{\mathrm{cone}}&C_{\mathrm{metric}}\sum_{j}|J_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}}{\mathcal{F}}^{-n_{*}}|_{*}\\
&\leqslant 2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}C_{\mathrm{cone}}C_{\mathrm{metric}}(1+n_{*}(B_{0}-1))\theta_{*}^{n_{*}}\leqslant\gamma/(Q_{3}2e^{\varpi_{2}}C_{\mathrm{cone}}).\end{split}

Here,
\delta_{n_{*}}
is given by Lemma
6.13
and we finally fix the parameter
\delta_{*}=\min\{\delta_{n_{*}},1\}
in the Definition (
4.1
) of standard curves. By (
6.14
) and (
6.15
),

(6.16)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:contraction-Z}\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{*}})\leqslant 2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}C_{\mathrm{cone}}C_{\mathrm{metric}}(1+n_{*}(B_{0}&-1))\theta_{*}^{n_{*}}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}})\leqslant\frac{\gamma}{Q_{3}2e^{\varpi_{2}}C_{\mathrm{cone}}}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}}).\end{split}

It remains to relate
\mathcal{Z}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{*}})
to
\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n_{*}}{\mathcal{G}})
. Let
\{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j,p}\}
a partition of
\widehat{W}_{k,j}^{n_{*}}
in components not bigger than
\delta_{*}
. These are the unstable curves defining
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n_{*}}{\mathcal{G}}
. We can ensure that for each
k,j
the number of derived components is less than
|\widehat{W}_{k,j}^{n_{*}}|/\delta_{*}+1
. As we remarked at the beginning of the proof
\max_{I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n_{*}}\rho/\min_{I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n_{*}}\rho\leqslant 2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}
and using that
\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j,p}\subseteq\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}
, we have

\begin{split}\int_{I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j,p}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n_{*}}\rho_{k}&\leqslant\max_{I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j,p}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n_{*}}\rho_{k}|I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j,p}}|\leqslant\max_{I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n_{*}}\rho_{k}|I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j,p}}|\leqslant 2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}\min_{I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n_{*}}\rho_{k}|I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j,p}}|\\
&\leqslant 2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}\frac{|I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j,p}}|}{|I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}}|}\int_{I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n_{*}}\rho_{k}.\end{split}

The expression above gives an estimate of the mass carried by each unstable curve in
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n_{*}}{\mathcal{G}}
after partitioning. Since
|I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j,p}}|\leqslant|\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j,p}|
and
|\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}|\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}|I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}}|
,

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}})&=\sum_{k}p_{k}\sum_{j}\sum_{p}\int_{I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j,p}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n_{*}}\rho_{k}\frac{1}{|\widehat{W}_{k,j,p}^{n_{*}}|}\\
&\leqslant 2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}\sum_{k}p_{k}\sum_{j}\sum_{p}\frac{|I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j,p}}|}{|I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}}|}\int_{I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n_{*}}\rho_{k}\frac{1}{|\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j,p}|}\\
&\leqslant 2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}C_{\mathrm{cone}}\sum_{k}p_{k}\sum_{j}\sum_{p}\frac{1}{|\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}|}\int_{I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n_{*}}\rho_{k}.\end{split}

Since
\sum_{p}1\leqslant|\widehat{W}_{k,j}|/\delta_{*}+1
, we obtain the following estimate,

\begin{split}&\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n_{*}}{\mathcal{G}})\leqslant 2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}C_{\mathrm{cone}}\sum_{k}p_{k}\sum_{j}\biggl(\frac{|\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}|}{\delta_{*}}+1\biggr)\frac{1}{|\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}|}\int_{I_{\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n_{*}}\rho_{k}\\
&\leqslant 2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}C_{\mathrm{cone}}\biggl[\sum_{k}p_{k}\sum_{j}\frac{1}{|\widehat{W}^{n_{*}}_{k,j}|}\int_{I_{\widehat{W}_{k,j}}}\rho_{k}+\delta_{*}^{-1}\sum_{k}p_{k}\sum_{j}\int_{I_{\widehat{W}_{k,j}}}\rho_{k}\biggr].\end{split}

Using the first equality in (
6.12
) and the fact that
\sum_{k}p_{k}\sum_{j}\int_{I_{\widehat{W}_{k,j}}}\rho_{k}=1
, we obtain

(6.17)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:chopping-estimate}\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n_{*}}{\mathcal{G}})\leqslant 2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}C_{\mathrm{cone}}\mathcal{Z}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{*}})+\frac{2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}C_{\mathrm{cone}}}{\delta_{*}}.

Therefore, by (
6.16
) and (
6.17
), setting
Z_{0}=\frac{2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}C_{\mathrm{cone}}}{\delta_{*}}
, we obtain that
\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n_{*}}{\mathcal{G}})\leqslant\gamma\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}})+Z_{0}
. As for the second part of the statement, by (
6.17
) and the first inequality in (
6.16
) with
1
in place of
n_{*}
we obtain

\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}{\mathcal{G}})\leqslant(2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}C_{\mathrm{cone}})^{2}C_{\mathrm{metric}}B_{0}\theta_{*}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}})+Z_{0}\leqslant\bigl((2e^{\varpi_{2}}Q_{3}C_{\mathrm{cone}})^{2}C_{\mathrm{metric}}B_{0}+Z_{0}\bigr)\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}}).

In the last inequality we have used that
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}})\geqslant 1
since each unstable curve is shorter than
\delta_{*}\leqslant 1
.
This implies that
\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}{\mathcal{G}})\leqslant Z_{1}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}})
for some
Z_{1}>0
big enough and concludes the proof of the statement.
∎

Let
n_{*}
,
Z_{0}
and
Z_{1}
be given by Lemma
6.14
and
\Omega>1
by Corollary
6.8
. Set also, for some
C_{\mathrm{cone}}>0
big enough,

(6.18)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:intrinsic-constant-boundary}\begin{split}&\mathbb{B}_{1}=Z_{0}/(1-\gamma),\quad\mathbb{B}_{2}=Z_{1}^{n_{*}}\mathbb{B}_{1}\quad\text{and}\\
&B_{2}=\max\{1000Z_{1}^{n_{*}}\max\{B_{1},\mathbb{B}_{1}\},10000e^{\Omega\varpi_{2}}C_{\mathrm{cone}}\mathbb{B}_{2}\}.\end{split}

Note that by Lemma
6.1
, (
6.18
) and (
6.8
) the constants
\varpi_{2},B_{2},D_{2}
depend only on the table and the initial constants
\varpi_{1},B_{1},D_{1}
, so that they can be incorporated in the generic constant
C
. Recall also the terminology introduced after Proposition
4.4
: We call
(\varpi_{1},B_{1},D_{1})
-standard families initial regular standard families and
(\varpi_{2},B_{2},D_{2})
-standard families regular standard families. Lemma
6.14
has the following Corollary, whose proof is a direct computation and it is omitted.

Corollary 6.15
.

There exist
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}>0
such that, for all
t\in[0,t_{0}]
and standard family
{\mathcal{G}}
with
\max\rho_{k}/\min\rho_{k}\leqslant 2e^{\varpi_{2}}
for every
k
, we have:

a)

If
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}})\leqslant\mathbb{B}_{1}
then
\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}})\leqslant\mathbb{B}_{2}
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
;

b)

\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}})\leqslant\max\{Z_{1}^{n_{*}}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}}),\mathbb{B}_{2}\}
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
;

c)

\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}})\leqslant\mathbb{B}_{2}
for all
n\geqslant\alpha_{1}\ln\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}})+\alpha_{2}
.

We now study the evolution
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
of the leaky system. To establish invariance, we use the contraction of
\mathcal{Z}
under
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n_{*}}
and the fact that after a finite number of iterations the lost mass is almost negligible for holes small enough. The following result is a weaker version of Lemma
4.6
(to obtain Lemma
4.6
we used invariance which we still haven’t proved).

Lemma 6.16
.

For any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exists
C_{n}>0
such that, for any regular standard family
{\mathcal{G}}
and any
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})\geqslant 1-C_{n}t.

Proof.

Using Lemma
6.11
in the last equality,

\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})=1-\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}(\cup_{k=0}^{n}{\mathcal{F}}^{-k}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}))\geqslant 1-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{F}}^{-k}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}))=1-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{F}}^{k}{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{H}}_{t}).

By part b) of Corollary
6.15
for
t=0
and because
{\mathcal{G}}
is a regular standard family, we have that
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{F}}^{k}{\mathcal{G}})\leqslant Z_{1}^{n_{*}}B_{2}
for any
k
and by point c) of Corollary
6.8
, we have that
{\mathcal{F}}^{k}{\mathcal{G}}
supports densities in
{\mathcal{C}}(\Omega\varpi_{2})
for all
k
. Therefore, by Lemma
4.3
and the equation above, for every
n>0
there exists
C_{n}>0
big enough such that, for all
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})\geqslant 1-\sum_{k=0}^{n}Ce^{\Omega\varpi_{2}}B_{2}t\geqslant 1-C_{n}t.

This concludes the proof of the statement.
∎

The next Lemma relates the evolution of the boundary for the open system to the evolution
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
with added discontinuities.

Lemma 6.17
.

For any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and standard family
{\mathcal{G}}
,

\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}})\leqslant\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}})}{\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}.

Proof.

To fix the notation, let
{\mathcal{G}}=\{(p_{k},W_{k},\rho_{k})\}
and
\{\widehat{W}_{k,j}\}
,
\{V_{k,j}\}
be the connected components of
W_{k}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}^{{\mathbb{H}}}
and
(W_{k}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}^{{\mathbb{H}}})\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}
respectively. Let also
\{\widehat{W}^{n}_{k,j}\}
and
\{V^{n}_{k,j}\}
be their
n^{th}
image (possibly further subdivided in curves of length less than
\delta_{*}
). Note that each element of the partition
\{V^{n}_{k,j}\}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}(W_{k}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})
is also an element of the partition
\{\widehat{W}^{n}_{k,j}\}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}(W_{k})
. Therefore,

\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}})=\sum_{k}p_{k}\sum_{j}\frac{\int_{V_{k,j}}\rho_{k}}{\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}\frac{1}{|V^{n}_{k,j}|}\leqslant\sum_{k}p_{k}\sum_{j}\frac{\int_{\widehat{W}_{k,j}}\rho_{k}}{\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}\frac{1}{|\widehat{W}^{n}_{k,j}|}=\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}})}{\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})},

where the first sums go over the elements of
\{V^{n}_{k,j}\}
and the second sums over those of
\{\widehat{W}^{n}_{k,j}\}
. This concludes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

We finally establish invariance for the leaky evolution. Recall that
(\varpi_{1},B_{1},D_{1})
-standard families are called initial regular standard families.

Proof of Proposition
4.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be an initial regular standard family. By Lemma
6.11
,
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}}=\mu_{{\mathcal{L}}^{n}_{t}{\mathcal{G}}}
. Hence the statement reduces to prove that
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}}
is a regular standard family for all
n
and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
with
t_{0}>0
small enough. Set
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}}=\{(p^{(n)}_{k},\rho^{(n)}_{k},W^{(n)}_{k})\}
. By construction the standard families
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}}
are composed by
n
-homogeneous unstable curves and the fact that
\rho^{(n)}_{k}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi_{2})
follows by part a) of Corollary
6.8
and the fact that
\rho_{k}^{(0)}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi_{1})
. This proves that the densities belong to the right cones. Moreover, it also shows that, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\frac{\max\rho^{(n)}_{k}}{\min\rho^{(n)}_{k}}\leqslant e^{\varpi_{2}\delta_{*}^{1/3}}\leqslant e^{\varpi_{2}}.

Hence, can apply Lemma
6.14
to the standard families
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}{\mathcal{G}}
. Let
n_{*}\in{\mathbb{N}}
be given by Lemma
6.14
. We first claim that for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{kn_{*}}{\mathcal{G}})\leqslant 10\max\{B_{1},Z_{0}/(1-\gamma)\}<B_{2}
. We prove the statement by induction. The case
k=0
follows from the fact that
{\mathcal{G}}
is an initial regular standard family. Assume now that the statement holds for some
k
. By the inductive assumption and Lemma
6.16
, there exists
C_{n_{*}}>0
such that, for all
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

(6.19)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:bound-lost-mass-invariance-today}\mu_{{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{kn_{*}}{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n_{*}})\geqslant 1-C_{n_{*}}t.

Therefore, by Lemmata
6.17
and
6.14
and the inductive assumption,

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{(k+1)n_{*}}{\mathcal{G}})\leqslant\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}_{t}^{n_{*}}{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{kn_{*}}{\mathcal{G}})}{\mu_{{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{kn_{*}}{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}&\leqslant\frac{\gamma 10\max\{B_{1},Z_{0}/(1-\gamma)\}+Z_{0}}{1-C_{n_{*}}t}\leqslant 10\max\{B_{1},Z_{0}/(1-\gamma)\}.\end{split}

In the last inequality in the equation above, we consider
t\in[0,t_{0}]
for
t_{0}>0
small enough depending on
C_{n_{*}}
. This is fine since
n_{*}
is fixed (and depends only on the billiard and the initial regularity data). This concludes the proof of the claim. For general
n=kn_{*}+q
,
q<n_{*}
, by Lemma
6.17
and (
6.19
) again, and for
t\in[0,t_{0}]
and
t_{0}>0
small enough,

(6.20)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:this-one}\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{kn_{*}+q}{\mathcal{G}})&\leqslant\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{q}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{kn_{*}}{\mathcal{G}}))}{\mu_{{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{kn_{*}}{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{q})}\leqslant\frac{Z_{1}^{q}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{kn_{*}}{\mathcal{G}})}{1-C_{n_{*}}t}\leqslant\frac{Z_{1}^{n_{*}}10\max\{B_{1},Z_{0}/(1-\gamma)\}}{1/2}\leqslant B_{2}.\end{split}

where in the second inequality we also used the second part of Lemma
6.14
. The last inequality follows from (
6.18
). This concludes the proof of the Proposition.
∎

6.4.
Coupling

In this section we define coupled standard families and we show that after a fixed iteration of the map we can couple two initial regular standard families.

Definition 6.18
.

Let
\ell_{1}=(W_{1},\rho_{1})
and
\ell_{2}=(W_{2},\rho_{2})
be two regular standard pairs and
\eta>0
. We say that
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
are
\eta
-coupled if

(6.21)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:eta-coupled-def}I_{W_{1}}=I_{W_{2}},\quad\|\varphi_{W_{1}}-\varphi_{W_{2}}\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}\leqslant\eta,\quad\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}.

Two standard families are
\eta
-coupled if there exists a bijection between their standard pairs so that each standard pair of the first family is
\eta
-coupled with the corresponding standard pair of the second family and the corresponding weights are equal. Moreover, if the first two conditions in (
6.21
) are satisfied for a pair of unstable curves
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
we say that
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
(or
\varphi_{W_{1}}
and
\varphi_{W_{2}}
) are
\eta
-stacked.

We start with a simple lemma which tracks the weights in standard families generated by a single standard pair. Intuitively, we want that a long curve in the push-forward carries at least some portion of the original mass. The dependence on
n
is not relevant as we need to couple only a small amount of mass but every fixed number of iterations.

Lemma 6.19
.

Let
\ell
be a regular standard pair and set
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}\ell=\{(p_{k},W_{k}^{\prime},\rho_{k})\}
. For any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists
c_{n}>0
such that
p_{k}\geqslant c_{n}|W_{k}^{\prime}|^{2^{n}}
.

Proof.

Call
\{W_{k}\}_{k}
the connected elements of
W\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{n,t}^{{\mathbb{H}}}
and
W_{k}^{\prime}={\mathcal{F}}^{n}(W_{k})
(if
|W_{k}^{\prime}|\geqslant\delta_{*}
we partition it in multiple components and this further subdivision does not affect the proof). According to (
6.9
) and the discussion afterwards,
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}\ell=\{(p_{k},W_{k}^{\prime},\rho_{k})\}
, where

p_{k}=\int_{I_{W_{k}}}\rho\geqslant e^{-\varpi_{2}}\frac{|I_{W_{k}}|}{|W|}\geqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}^{-1}e^{-\varpi_{2}}|W_{k}|.

In the first inequality we used that
\ell
supports a regular density and in the second that
|W|\leqslant 1
. By Lemma
3.1
, for some
C_{n}>0
,

|W_{k}^{\prime}|=|{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(W_{k})|\leqslant C_{n}|W_{k}|^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}\quad\text{or}\quad|W_{k}|\geqslant C_{n}^{-1}|W_{k}^{\prime}|^{2^{n}}

This, together with the equation before, concludes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

We introduce partitions
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
which are instrumental for our coupling arguments. Let
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
be a partition of
{\mathcal{M}}
into squares, each one of size
\eta>0
, whose sides are parallel to the
r
and
\varphi
coordinates axes. Here and in the following the size of a square is the length of its sides. For any unstable curve
W
we denote by

(6.22)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:center-unstable-curve-def}\begin{split}\text{Center}(W)=\biggl\{x\in W:&\text{ the segment in $I_{W}$ connecting}\\
&\hskip 79.6678pt\text{$\pi_{r}(x)$ to $\pi_{r}(\partial W)$ is longer than }\frac{|I_{W}|}{100e^{\varpi_{2}}}\biggr\}.\end{split}

Set also

(6.23)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:setDeltastar}\Delta_{*}=\frac{1}{100\mathbb{B}_{2}}.

In view of part (c) of Corollary
6.15
,
\Delta_{*}
is a lower bound on the length that most (
\geqslant 99\%
) unstable curves have at equilibrium. Note that
\Delta_{*}\gg 1/B_{2}
. More precisely, by (
6.18
),

(6.24)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:relationDelta-B2}\Delta_{*}\geqslant\frac{100C_{\mathrm{cone}}e^{\varpi_{2}}}{B_{2}}.

This means that we have been quite loose in defining a standard family regular as at equilibrium standard families are actually much better than
B_{2}
-regular. In Lemma
6.23
we couple a fraction of two standard families. The next three Lemmata are preliminary results that we comment on the way. First, we show that it is possible to
\eta
-stack long curves for
\eta
arbitrarily small if they intersect a square in
{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N}
. The general idea is that the sets
{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N}
are good approximations of the unstable manifolds when
N\to\infty
and, if two long curves happen to be in the same connected component of
{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N}
, at least part of them is close in the
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
norm.

Lemma 6.20
.

For any
\eta>0
there exists
N>0
such that if the centers of two
N
-homogeneous unstable curves
W_{\iota}
with
|W_{\iota}|\geqslant\Delta_{*}
intersect the same square
Q\subset{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N}
then there exist two
\eta
-stacked curves
W_{\iota,c}\subseteq W_{\iota}
with
|W_{\iota,c}|\geqslant 1/B_{2}
.

Proof.

First notice that by sending
N\to\infty
we have that
\mathrm{diam}(Q)\to 0
for any
Q\subset{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N}
. Indeed, if not,
Q\subset{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N}
would contain a stable manifold of a certain fixed size, which is absurd when
N
approaches infinity. Therefore, we let
N
be large enough so that
\mathrm{diam}(Q)\ll\Delta_{*}
for any
Q\subset{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N}
. Because both curves
W_{\iota}
are aligned with
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{u}
their slopes
\varphi_{W_{\iota}}^{\prime}
are bounded from above and below, i.e., for some
C_{\mathrm{cone}}>0
,

(6.25)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:too-many-cones}C_{\mathrm{cone}}^{-1}\leqslant|\varphi_{W_{\iota}}^{\prime}|\leqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}.

Since
|W_{\iota}|\geqslant\Delta_{*}
,
\text{Center}(W_{\iota})\cap Q\neq\emptyset
and
\mathrm{diam}(Q)\ll\Delta_{*}
, there exists an interval
I_{c}
such that
I_{W_{1}}\cap I_{W_{2}}\supseteq I_{c}
. Denote by
\varphi_{\iota,c}=\varphi_{W_{\iota}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{I_{c}}
and by
W_{\iota,c}
the graphs of
\varphi_{\iota,c}
. These are the stacked curves. By (
6.25
) the
r
-projections of the curves are long at least
|I_{W_{\iota}}|\geqslant\Delta_{*}/C_{\mathrm{cone}}
and by (
6.22
) each
I_{W_{\iota}}
extends beyond
\pi_{r}(Q)
on both sides by
\Delta_{*}/(C_{\mathrm{cone}}100e^{\varpi_{2}})
. In particular, we can require that the stacked curves are longer than

(6.26)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:geom-square-coupled}|I_{W_{\iota,c}}|\geqslant\frac{\Delta_{*}}{100C_{\mathrm{cone}}e^{\varpi_{2}}}\quad\text{so that}\quad|W_{\iota,c}|\geqslant\frac{\Delta_{*}}{100C_{\mathrm{cone}}e^{\varpi_{2}}}\geqslant\frac{1}{B_{2}},

where in the second inequality on the right we used (
6.24
). It remains to prove that
\varphi_{\iota,c}
are
\eta
-stacked if
N
is big enough. Since both curves intersect the same square
Q\subset{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N}
and are
N
-homogeneous, they belong to the same connected component of
{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N}
. Let
r_{0}\in I_{c}
and
\eta^{\prime}>0
be such that
|\varphi_{1,c}(r_{0})-\varphi_{2,c}(r_{0})|=\eta^{\prime}
and denote by
H_{0}
the vertical line connecting
(r_{0},\varphi_{1,c}(r_{0}))
to
(r_{0},\varphi_{2,c}(r_{0}))
. By Lemma
3.3
about continuation of singularity lines, we have that
H_{0}\subseteq{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N}
. Therefore,
{\mathcal{F}}^{-N}(H_{0})
is a (continuous) stable curve and its length is at most of order one, as it cannot exceed the phase space. On the other hand, since
H_{0}
is aligned with the large stable cone, by the expansion (
3.8
) of stable curves in the past,

|{\mathcal{F}}^{-N}(H_{0})|\geqslant C\Lambda_{0}^{N}|H_{0}|\geqslant C\Lambda_{0}^{N}\eta^{\prime}.

Hence, the equation above implies that
\eta^{\prime}\lesssim\Lambda_{0}^{-N}
. Therefore, by choosing
N
large enough, we obtain that
\|\varphi_{1,c}-\varphi_{2,c}\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{0}}\leqslant\eta
. As for the bound on the difference of the slopes, for any
r_{0}\in I_{c}
, we have (see
[
21
, Equations (3.34) and (3.39)]
and notice that
{\mathcal{C}}^{u}
corresponds to wave fronts with
0\leqslant{\mathcal{B}}\leqslant\infty
)

(6.27)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:slope-vector-push-cone}\frac{\cos\varphi_{\iota,c}(r_{0})}{\tau_{-1}+\frac{1}{{\mathcal{R}}_{-1}+\frac{1}{\ddots+\frac{1}{{\mathcal{R}}_{-N+1}}}}}+\kappa(r_{0})\leqslant\varphi_{\iota,c}^{\prime}(r_{0})\leqslant\frac{\cos\varphi_{\iota,c}(r_{0})}{\tau_{-1}+\frac{1}{{\mathcal{R}}_{-1}+\frac{1}{\ddots+\frac{1}{\tau_{-N}}}}}+\kappa(r_{0}),

where
{\mathcal{R}}=2\kappa/\cos\varphi
and the negative subscripts indicate that the quantities are evaluated along the backward orbit of
x_{\iota}=(r_{0},\varphi_{\iota,c}(r_{0}))
. Calling
{\mathcal{B}}_{\iota,N}^{e}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{\iota,N}^{o}
the continued fraction on the right and on the left, we have (see
[
21
, Lemma 4.36]
)

|{\mathcal{B}}_{\iota,N}^{e}-{\mathcal{B}}_{\iota,N}^{o}|\leqslant\frac{1}{N\tau_{\mathrm{min}}}.

Therefore, we pick
M>0
big enough
M<N
so that
\varphi_{\iota,r}^{\prime}
is approximated arbitrarily well on
{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-M}
by the truncations
\cos\varphi_{\iota,c}{\mathcal{B}}_{\iota,M}^{e}+\kappa
, which we now prove to be arbitrarily close at the two points the points
(r_{0},\varphi_{\iota,c}(r_{0}))
. Note that
\cos\varphi_{\iota,c}{\mathcal{B}}_{\iota,M}^{e}+\kappa
are uniformly continuous functions on
{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N}\subset{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-M}
,
N\gg M
, since they involve backward iterates until time
-M
. Moreover, by the first part of the Lemma, the points
(r_{0},\varphi_{\iota,c}(r_{0}))
can be made arbitrarily close by choosing
N
big enough. Therefore, by continuity, the function
\cos\varphi_{\iota,c}{\mathcal{B}}_{\iota,M}^{e}+\kappa
takes values arbitrarily close at the points
(r_{0},\varphi_{\iota,c}(r_{0}))
when
N\to\infty
. This concludes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

Once two curves are stacked, it is possible to couple a portion of the mass they carry.

Lemma 6.21
.

There exists
p_{c}>0
such that, for any two
W_{1},W_{2}\in\mathcal{W}
as in Lemma
6.20
and standard pairs
\ell_{\iota}=(W_{\iota},\rho_{\iota})
with
\rho_{\iota}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi_{2}/2)
, there exist two
\eta
-coupled regular standard pairs
\ell_{\iota,c}
and two regular standard families
{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota,u}
such that

\mu_{\ell_{\iota}}=p_{c}\mu_{\ell_{\iota,c}}+(1-p_{c})\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota,u}}.

Proof.

The fact that there exist
\eta
-stacked unstable curves
W_{\iota,c}\subseteq W_{\iota}
follows by Lemma
6.20
. Since
\rho_{\iota}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi_{2}/2)
, by Lemma
4.1
,

\rho_{\iota}\geqslant\frac{e^{-\varpi_{2}/2}}{|W_{\iota}|}\geqslant\frac{e^{-\varpi_{2}}}{\delta_{*}}\geqslant e^{-\varpi_{2}}:=c_{0}.

Let
\rho_{*}:I_{c}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
be the constant function equal to
c_{0}/4
. By Lemma
6.10
and because
\rho_{\iota}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi_{2}/2)
, we have that
(\rho_{\iota}-c_{0}/4){\mathbbm{1}}_{I_{c}}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi_{2})
. We define the coupled standard pairs and the coupled mass as

\ell_{\iota,c}=\biggl(W_{\iota,c},\frac{\rho_{*}}{\int_{I_{c}}\rho_{*}}\biggr)\quad\text{and}\quad p_{c}=\int_{I_{c}}\rho_{*}=c_{0}|I_{c}|\geqslant c_{0}C_{\mathrm{cone}}^{-1}B_{2}^{-1}\geqslant C_{\mathrm{cone}}^{-1}\frac{e^{-\varpi_{2}}}{B_{2}},

where in the first inequality we have used that, by Lemma
6.20
, it holds
|W_{\iota,c}|\geqslant 1/B_{2}
. This also shows that the standard pairs
\ell_{\iota,c}
are regular standard pairs. Moreover, by (
6.26
) and by coupling less mass if necessary, we have that
|W_{\iota,c}|\leqslant\Delta_{*}/(50C_{\mathrm{cone}}e^{\varpi_{2}})
so that
|W_{\iota}\setminus W_{\iota,c}|\gg 1/B_{2}
. This, together with the fact that
(\rho_{\iota}-c_{0}/4){\mathbbm{1}}_{I_{c}}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi_{2})
, shows that the uncoupled standard family is regular as well.
∎

We will use the next result to show that the evolution of curves actually intersects the small squares
Q\subset{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N}
of before. The next Lemma is a direct consequence of the known equidistribution of standard pairs for the billiard map, but notably similar statements of ‘finite mixing for curves’ can be deduced by the mixing of the billiard map (after much more effort of course. See e.g.
[
21
, Proposition 7.83]
. Note that the statement below isn’t an
immediate
consequence of mixing just because standard pairs correspond to singular measures.)

Lemma 6.22
.

For any
\eta>0
there exists
N>0
such that, for all
n\geqslant N
, any regular standard pair
\ell
and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, we have

\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}}\bigl|\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{t}(\ell)}({\mathbbm{1}}_{Q})-\mu_{0}(Q)\bigr|\leqslant\frac{1}{100}.

Proof.

By
[
21
, Theorem 7.31]
(and approximating
{\mathbbm{1}}_{Q}
with
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
functions), we have that for all
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
, there exists
n_{Q}
such that for all
n\geqslant n_{Q}
,

|\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}(\ell)}({\mathbbm{1}}_{Q})-\mu_{0}(Q)|=|\mu_{\ell}({\mathbbm{1}}_{Q}\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{n})-\mu_{0}(Q)|\leqslant\frac{\mu_{0}(Q)}{100}.

The first equality follows from Lemma
6.11
. Since there are a finite number of elements
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
, the statement follows by taking
N=\max_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}}n_{Q}<\infty
and the fact that
\mu_{0}
is a probability measure.
∎

Later on, we will fix
\eta_{0}\ll\Delta_{*}
to be a very small number. This is the threshold distance that two standard pairs need to have to be meaningfully considered coupled. The precise value of
\eta_{0}
is given by Lemma
6.39
and depends only on the billiard and the initial regularity of standard families. In practice, we want
\eta_{0}
to be small enough that not all the mass gets decoupled in a few iterations. In the following Lemma we argue that it is actually possible to couple two standard families.

Lemma 6.23
.

For any
\eta_{0}>0
there exists
N>0
such that the following is true. For any
n\geqslant N
, there exist
t_{0}>0
,
p_{c},p_{\iota,u}\in(0,1)
,
p_{c}+p_{\iota,u}\leqslant 1
with the following property. For any two regular standard families
{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota}
there exist two
\eta_{0}
-coupled regular standard pairs
\ell_{\iota,c}
and two regular standard families
{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota,u}
such that, for any
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}({\mathcal{M}})
and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota}}(\phi\circ\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{t}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})=p_{c}\mu_{\ell_{\iota,c}}(\phi)+p_{\iota,u}\mu_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota,u}}(\phi).

To help the reader, we give an informal description before proceeding with the actual proof. We first let
N_{**}>0
be big enough such that we can apply Lemma
6.20
and two curves that intersect a square in
{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N_{**}}
can be
\eta_{0}
-stacked. We consider a partition
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta_{1}}
of
{\mathcal{M}}
in squares so small that most of them belong to
{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N_{**}}
. We then pick
N\geqslant N_{**}
big enough so that
n\geqslant N
satisfies two properties. The first is that
\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}\ell_{\iota})
is at equilibrium. Indeed, by (
6.24
) regular standard families are allowed to have a boundary much bigger (
\sim B_{2}
) than what they tend to have (i.e., roughly
Z_{0}/(1-\gamma)
) after enough iterations. The second property is that plenty of long curves in
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}\ell_{\iota}
intersect most of the little squares in
Q\subset{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N_{**}}
. This follows by mixing of the billiard map via Lemma
6.22
. Doing so, most curves will be long and equidistributed in the phase space: it is impossible that there are no couple of curves, one from the first family and one from the second, that do not intersect the same little square in
Q\subset{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N_{**}}
. Once
n
is fixed, we pick
t_{0}>0
very small, so that the mass that ends up in the hole in the first
n
iterates is almost negligible.

Proof of Lemma
6.23
.

We first consider the case in which the two standard families are given by single standard pairs
\ell_{\iota}=(W_{\iota},\rho_{\iota})
. Fix some
\eta_{0}>0
. By Corollary (
6.15
) point (c) and the fact that
\mathcal{Z}(\ell_{\iota})\leqslant B_{2}
, there exits
N_{*}\sim\ln B_{2}
such that, for any
n\geqslant N_{*}
,
\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}\ell_{\iota})\leqslant\mathbb{B}_{2}
. Hence, setting
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{t}\ell_{\iota}=\{(p_{\iota,k},W_{\iota,k},\rho_{\iota,k})\}
, by Lemma
6.12
and (
6.23
), for any
n\geqslant N_{*}
,

(6.28)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:most-curves-are-long-image}\sum_{k:|W_{\iota,k}|<\Delta_{*}}p_{\iota,k}\leqslant\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}\ell)\Delta_{*}\leqslant\mathbb{B}_{2}\Delta_{*}=\frac{1}{100}.

Let
N_{**}>0
be given by Lemma
6.20
with
\eta=\eta_{0}
and let
\eta_{1}>0
be such that the overwhelming majority of squares in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta_{1}}
belong to
{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N_{**}}
. This is possible since
{\mathcal{S}}_{-N_{**}}
is a finite union of
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
curves. In particular, we take
\eta_{1}>0
small enough such that

(6.29)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:eta1-squares}\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta_{1}}:Q\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{-N_{**}}\neq\emptyset}\mu_{0}(Q)\leqslant\frac{1}{100}

Let also
N_{***}\in{\mathbb{N}}
be given by Lemma
6.22
with
\eta=\eta_{1}
. Finally, let
\overline{n}
be given by part b) of Corollary
6.8
. Set
N=\max\{N_{*},N_{**},N_{***},\overline{n}\}
and fix some
n\geqslant N
. We now define the set of squares
{\mathcal{Q}}_{good}(\iota)
where we can couple mass. Let

\begin{split}N_{t}(\iota)&=\biggl\{x\in W_{\iota}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}:\text{ }\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}(x)\text{ belongs to the Center of an unstable curve in $\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}\ell$ }\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\text{ not smaller than }\Delta_{*}\biggr\}.\end{split}

These are the points that survive after
n
iterations and belong to the center of long unstable curves. We also set

{\mathcal{Q}}_{good}(\iota)=\{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta_{1}}:Q\cap{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(N_{t}(\iota))\neq\emptyset\text{ and }Q\subset{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N_{**}}\}.

We first prove first the following fact.
Claim
(\star)
: there exists
t_{0}>0
small enough such that

\text{ for any $t\in[0,t_{0}]$, }{\mathcal{Q}}_{good}(1)\cap{\mathcal{Q}}_{good}(2)\neq\emptyset.

If
Q\notin Q_{good}(\iota)
, either
Q\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{-N_{**}}\neq\emptyset
or
Q\cap{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(N_{t}(\iota))=\emptyset
. Therefore,

\begin{split}&\sum_{Q\notin{\mathcal{Q}}_{good}(\iota)}\mu_{0}(Q)=\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta_{1}}:Q\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{-N_{**}}\neq\emptyset}\mu_{0}(Q)+\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta_{1}}:Q\cap{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(N_{t}(\iota))=\emptyset}\mu_{0}(Q)\\
&\leqslant\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta_{1}}:Q\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{-N_{**}}\neq\emptyset}\mu_{0}(Q)+\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta_{1}}}\bigl|\mu_{0}(Q)-\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{t}(\ell_{\iota})}({\mathbbm{1}}_{Q})\bigr|+\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta_{1}}:Q\cap{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(N_{t}(\iota))=\emptyset}\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{t}(\ell_{\iota})}({\mathbbm{1}}_{Q}).\end{split}

By Lemma
6.22
and Equation (
6.29
), the first two sums contribute to less than
1/100
each. For the third sum, since
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta_{1}}
is a partition of
{\mathcal{M}}
,

\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta_{1}}:Q\cap{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(N_{t}(\iota))=\emptyset}\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}(\ell_{i})}({\mathbbm{1}}_{Q})\leqslant\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}(\ell_{\iota})}({\mathcal{M}}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}(N_{t}(\iota))).

Recall the notation
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{t}\ell_{\iota}=\{(p_{\iota,k},W_{\iota,k},\rho_{\iota,k})\}
. We note that the points in
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}(W_{\iota})\cap({\mathcal{M}}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}(N_{t}(\iota)))
are those points that either belong to small components, or belong to long components but not in their centers, or end up in the hole. Therefore, according to the previous equation,

\begin{split}\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta_{1}}:Q\cap{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(N_{t}(\iota))=\emptyset}\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}(\ell_{i})}({\mathbbm{1}}_{Q})&\leqslant\sum_{k:|W_{\iota,k}|<\Delta_{*}}p_{\iota,k}+\sum_{k:|W_{\iota,k}|\geqslant\Delta_{*}}p_{\iota,k}\mu_{\ell_{\iota,k}}({\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\text{Center}W_{\iota,k}})\\
&+\mu_{\ell_{\iota}}({\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}).\end{split}

We estimate each contribution one by one. By equation (
6.28
), the first term is less than
1/100
. By (
6.22
) and the fact that
\rho_{\iota,k}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi_{2})
,

\mu_{\ell_{\iota,k}}({\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}\setminus\text{Center}W_{\iota,k}})=\int_{I_{W_{\iota,k}\setminus\text{Center}W_{\iota,k}}}\rho_{\iota,k}\leqslant\frac{e^{\varpi_{2}}}{|I_{W_{\iota,k}}|}|I_{W_{\iota,k}\setminus\text{Center}W_{\iota,k}}|\leqslant\frac{2}{100}.

This estimate holds for each standard pair, giving a
2/100
estimate for the second term. Finally, by Lemma
6.16
and for
t_{0}>0
small enough depending on
n
, and
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

\mu_{\ell_{\iota}}({\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})\leqslant C_{n}t\leqslant\frac{1}{100}.

Therefore, collecting the previous estimates, we have, for both
\iota\in\{1,2\}
,

\sum_{Q\notin{\mathcal{Q}}_{good}(\iota)}\mu_{0}(Q)\leqslant\frac{6}{100}<\frac{1}{2}.

In particular, the equation above proves Claim
(\star)
. Let
Q_{c}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{good}(1)\cap{\mathcal{Q}}_{good}(2)
. By definition of good squares we have that a long unstable curve
\widetilde{W}_{1}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}(W_{1}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})
and a long unstable curve
\widetilde{W}_{2}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}(W_{2}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})
intersect the same square
Q_{c}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta_{1}}
. Consider the standard pairs

\widetilde{\ell}_{\iota}=\biggl(\widetilde{W}_{\iota},\frac{{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho_{\iota|I_{\widetilde{W}_{\iota}}}}{\int_{I_{\widetilde{W}_{\iota}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho}\biggr).

By part b) of Corollary
6.8
,
{\mathcal{L}}^{n}\rho_{\iota|I_{\widetilde{W}_{\iota}}}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi_{2}/2)
. Moreover, by construction
\widetilde{W}_{\iota}
are
N_{**}
-homogeneous (because they belong to
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}\ell_{\iota}
for
n\geqslant N_{**}
), they are long in the sense that
|\widetilde{W}_{\iota}|\geqslant\Delta_{*}
and they intersect the same square
Q_{c}\subset{\mathcal{M}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{-N_{**}}
. Therefore, by Lemma
6.20
, there exist
\eta_{0}
-stacked curves
W_{\iota,c}\subseteq\widetilde{W}_{\iota}
and, applying Lemma
6.21
, there exists
\widetilde{p}_{c}>0
, two regular standard families
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota,u}
and regular
\eta_{0}
-coupled standard pairs
\ell_{\iota,c}
such that

\mu_{\widetilde{\ell}_{\iota}}=\widetilde{p}_{c}\mu_{\ell_{\iota,c}}+(1-\widetilde{p}_{c})\mu_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota,u}}.

Since the curves
\widetilde{W}_{\iota}
belongs to
{\mathcal{F}}^{n}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})
, the decomposition above implies that, for some
\widetilde{p}_{\iota},p_{\iota,u},p_{\iota,u}^{\prime}>0
and standard families
{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota,u},{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota,u}^{\prime}
, for any
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}({\mathcal{M}})
,

(6.30)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:coupled-uncoupled-dec-first-meeting}\mu_{\ell_{\iota}}(\phi\circ\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}})=\widetilde{p}_{\iota}\mu_{\widetilde{\ell}_{\iota}}(\phi)+p_{\iota,u}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota,u}}(\phi)=\widetilde{p}_{c}\min_{\iota\in\{1,2\}}\widetilde{p}_{\iota}\mu_{\ell_{\iota,c}}(\phi)+p_{\iota,u}^{\prime}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota,u}^{\prime}}(\phi).

Here,
{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota,u}^{\prime}
is a convex combination of
{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota,u}
and
\ell_{\iota,c}
. By Lemma
6.19
, for some
c_{n}>0
,

p_{c}:=\widetilde{p}_{c}\min_{\iota\in\{1,2\}}\widetilde{p}_{\iota}\geqslant c_{n}\widetilde{p}_{c}(\Delta_{*})^{2^{n}}>0,

yielding a lower bound on the amount of coupled mass that is uniform in the choice of the initial standard pairs. To conclude the Lemma we need to bound the regularity of
{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota,u}^{\prime}
. The fact that the densities are in
{\mathcal{C}}(\varpi_{2})
follows from the fact that
{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota,u}
has that property by Lemma
6.21
and
\ell_{\iota,c}
is regular. Moreover, by (
6.30
) and the equation after it,

(6.31)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:estimate-reg-uncoupled}\mu_{{\mathcal{L}}_{t}^{n}\ell_{\iota}}=\frac{p_{c}}{\mu_{\ell_{\iota}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}\mu_{\ell_{\iota,c}}+\frac{p_{\iota,u}^{\prime}}{\mu_{\ell_{\iota}}({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n})}\mu_{{\mathcal{G}}_{\iota,u}^{\prime}}

By Lemmata
6.17
and
6.16
and recalling the choice of
N_{*}
before (
6.28
), there exists
t_{0}>0
small enough depending on
n
such that, for
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,
